# Wu-Xing Dynamics Project — Complete Record of the Work

**Authors / collaborators:** Carter Feng, Yunjiao Wang
**Subject:** dynamics of the Chinese-medicine five-element (Wu-Xing, 五行) organ network — the bilinear Lin & Han (2020) model versus a saturating model, with stability analysis, bifurcation analysis, and a pathology section.

---

## How to read this document

This file is a record of the whole working session, in the order the work happened. For every analysis it gives four things, in this order:

1. **What we were trying to do and why** (plain language),
2. **The code**, copied *verbatim* from the files that produced the results (notebook cells or scripts) — nothing is paraphrased or shortened,
3. **How each figure is constructed** (what is plotted on each axis, what is computed to colour it, what to look for), and the figure itself,
4. **The calculated values** — the numbers printed when the code was **re-run from scratch while this document was being written**, so every number below was produced by the code above it, not copied from memory.

All figures are in the `figures/` folder next to this file; they are the fresh outputs of that same re-run.

### How the numbers were produced (honesty notes)

* Every notebook was converted to a plain script and executed again end-to-end; its printed output is captured below each code cell. Figures are the images those runs saved.
* Where the original verification scripts had been lost between working sessions (`verify*.py`, `derive.py`, `path.py`), I re-created them from the logic described in the conversation; they are included in full and labelled **"re-created"**. Their numbers can differ in the last digits from the ones I quoted earlier in the conversation; where they do, the fresh number is the one to use and the difference is called out.
* One helper (`path.py`, which defined the four index-map functions) was missing, so `make_pathology_figures.py` could not run as saved. I replaced the missing `exec(open('path.py')…)` line with the four function definitions (they are simply `(i-1)%5`, `(i+1)%5`, `(i-2)%5`, `(i+2)%5`). The saved copies in `paper_v2/` and `paper_v3/` still contain the old line; the version printed in this document is the fixed one.
* Random-number seeds are fixed everywhere, so rerunning the code reproduces the numbers exactly.

# Part 0 — The two models

### 0.1 The network

Five organs, indexed 1…5 (in code 0…4) in the *generating* (Sheng, 生) order:

| index | element | organ |
|---|---|---|
| x1 | Wood 木 | Liver |
| x2 | Fire 火 | Heart |
| x3 | Earth 土 | Spleen |
| x4 | Metal 金 | Lung |
| x5 | Water 水 | Kidney |

Two relations live on this ring:

* **Sheng (generating) cycle** — each organ nourishes the next: Wood→Fire→Earth→Metal→Water→Wood.
* **Ke (controlling) cycle** — each organ restrains the one two steps ahead: Wood⊣Earth, Fire⊣Metal, Earth⊣Water, Metal⊣Wood, Water⊣Fire (the "pentagram").

So every organ has exactly four neighbours, and the code uses indices modulo 5:

| role of the neighbour (relative to organ *i*) | index | `np.roll` form |
|---|---|---|
| **mother** — generates *i* (Sheng) | i−1 | `np.roll(x, 1)` |
| **controller** — restrains *i* (Ke) | i−2 | `np.roll(x, 2)` |
| **child** — is generated by *i* | i+1 | `np.roll(x, -1)` |
| **target** — is restrained by *i* | i+2 | `np.roll(x, -2)` |

(`np.roll(x, 1)` shifts the array right by one, so entry *i* of the rolled array is `x[i-1]` — the mother.)

### 0.2 The Lin & Han (2020) bilinear model

Lotka–Volterra / mass-action ("Holling type I") couplings:

$$\dot x_i = a x_i - b x_i^2 + c\,x_i x_{i-1} - d\,x_i x_{i-2} - g\,x_i x_{i+1} - h\,x_i x_{i+2}$$

| coefficient | meaning |
|---|---|
| a | self-generation |
| b | self-saturation |
| c | gain from the mother (isG) |
| d | restraint from the controller (isR) |
| g | cost of generating the child (G) |
| h | cost of restraining the target (R) |

Balanced (all-equal) state: setting all x_i = x\* gives x\*(a − M x\*) = 0 with **M = b + d + g + h − c**, so x\* = a/M, which exists only if M > 0. If M < 0 the symmetric subspace obeys ẋ = a x + |M| x² and **blows up in finite time**.

### 0.3 The saturating model (ours)

Same graph; couplings saturate (a "type II" response):

$$\dot x_i = a x_i - k x_i^2 + \frac{c\,x_{i-1}}{1+x_i} - \frac{g\,x_i}{1+x_{i+2}} - h\,x_i x_{i-2}$$

* **Sheng** nourishment `c x_mother/(1+x_i)` — the mother supplies the organ, but the supply is *demand-limited*: a full organ (large x_i) draws less.
* **Ke over-acting** `g x_i/(1+x_target)` — saturating in the target.
* **Ke restraint** `h x_i x_controller` — kept bilinear (restraint is proportional to both).

Balanced state: all x_i = x\* with  (k+h)x² + [(k+h) − a]x − (a + c − g) = 0. At the baseline a=2, k=0.5, c=1, g=0.5, h=0.3 this gives x\* = 3/4 + √59/4 ≈ 2.670286.

A positively invariant, attracting box [0, X_max]⁵ exists with X_max = (a + √(a² + 4kc))/(2k): the saturating model cannot blow up.

### 0.4 Extended model (used in the pathology section)

$$\dot x_i = a_i x_i - k x_i^2 + \frac{c\,x_{i-1}}{1+x_i} - \frac{g_d\,x_{i+1}x_i}{1+x_i} - \frac{g\,x_i}{1+x_{i+2}} - h\,x_i x_{i-2} - \frac{b\,x_{i+2}}{1+\varepsilon x_i}$$

with a per-organ self-generation a_i (so one organ can be made deficient or excessive), a **demand term** g_d (a child draws qi from its mother) and a **Wu / rebellion term** b (a weakened organ is counter-attacked by its Ke target).


# Part 1 — XPPAUT files and bifurcation questions

**Your request (first message):** you pasted `chineseOrgans.tex` and `main2.tex` and asked for XPPAUT `.ode` files for several variants: (1) ignore the back-draw ("backtrack of usage of element at first"), (2) or alternatively make that coefficient about 10× smaller, (3) the same for the case where an element is weak and so *affects the element that suppresses it* (the Wu / rebellion effect — a variant without it), (4) a node analysis of only two organs, (5) completely decoupled, because you do the analysis in XPPAUT and need `.ode` files; also a variant with an adaptive self-regulation. You asked me to start by writing and describing how the model should be.

What I produced: a model description, then six `.ode` files. The description came first, as requested.


## 1.1 The model description (as delivered)

### Wu-Xing Five-Organ ODE Model: Full Description
#### For XPP-AUT Analysis

---

#### 1. State Variables

| Variable | Element | Organ |
|----------|---------|-------|
| x1 | Wood (木) | Liver (肝) |
| x2 | Fire (火) | Heart (心) |
| x3 | Earth (土) | Spleen (脾) |
| x4 | Metal (金) | Lung (肺) |
| x5 | Water (水) | Kidney (肾) |

Each x_i(t) ≥ 0 represents the **functional qi (activity level)** of organ i.

---

#### 2. Cycle Mappings

##### Sheng (生, Generating) cycle — mother nourishes child:
    x1 → x2 → x3 → x4 → x5 → x1

So the **Sheng mother** σ(i) of each organ is:
    σ(1)=5,  σ(2)=1,  σ(3)=2,  σ(4)=3,  σ(5)=4

##### Ke (克, Controlling) cycle — the pentagram suppression:
    x1 → x3 → x5 → x2 → x4 → x1

So:
- **Ke target** κ(i): the organ that i controls
    κ(1)=3,  κ(2)=4,  κ(3)=5,  κ(4)=1,  κ(5)=2
- **Ke controller** ρ(i): the organ that controls i
    ρ(1)=4,  ρ(2)=5,  ρ(3)=1,  ρ(4)=2,  ρ(5)=3

---

#### 3. Term-by-Term Description

Each equation has the following structure:

    ẋ_i = [self-regulation] + [Sheng nourishment] - [Ke over-acting] - [Ke restraint] - [Wu rebellious]

##### (A) Self-regulation: `a·x_i - k·x_i²`
- Logistic growth: organ produces qi at rate a proportional to current level
- Quadratic damping k prevents unbounded growth; natural set point ≈ a/k
- **In the adaptive model**, this becomes:
    `k·x_i·(θ_i - x_i)` where θ_i = θ₀ + α·x_{σ(i)} - β·x_{ρ(i)}`
  which expands to:
    `a·x_i - k·x_i² + k·α·x_{σ(i)}·x_i - k·β·x_{ρ(i)}·x_i`
  (the set point shifts based on how much the mother is nourishing and how much the controller is suppressing)

##### (B) Sheng nourishment: `+c·x_{σ(i)} / (1 + x_i)`
- The mother organ x_{σ(i)} nourishes child x_i
- The saturation denominator `(1+x_i)` captures the **back-draw** (child demand):
  when x_i is low (child is weak/deficient), the nourishment rate is high —
  the mother works harder to replenish the child
- **Without back-draw** (Model B): this term becomes simply `+c·x_{σ(i)}`
  or with reduced coefficient `+(c/10)·x_{σ(i)}`

##### (C) Ke over-acting: `-g·x_i / (1 + x_{κ(i)})`
- x_{κ(i)} is the organ that x_i *controls* (x_i suppresses it)
- This term represents the **drain** on x_i due to the over-acting relationship:
  when the controlled organ x_{κ(i)} is low (deficient/weak), x_i cannot easily
  transfer its controlling energy there, so x_i itself suffers more drain
- **When the weak element is affected by its suppressor**:
  if x_{κ(i)} is weak, the organ that suppresses x_{κ(i)} (which is x_i)
  is *also* affected — this is already partially captured, but we can add a
  direct term: `-g_weak·x_i·(1/(1+x_{κ(i)}))`

##### (D) Ke restraint: `-h·x_i·x_{ρ(i)}`
- The Ke controller x_{ρ(i)} directly suppresses x_i
- This is the standard **competition** (相乘, Xiang Cheng) term:
  normal suppression proportional to both parties

##### (E) Wu (侮, Rebellious Counter-Acting): `-b·x_{κ(i)} / (1 + ε·x_i)`
- Under pathological conditions, when x_i becomes severely deficient (x_i small),
  the organ x_i *normally controls* (its Ke target x_{κ(i)}) **rebels back** against x_i
- The saturation form `1/(1+ε·x_i)` means: the smaller x_i, the stronger the
  rebellious drain on x_i from x_{κ(i)}
- **Without Wu terms** (Model D): remove all `-b·...` terms entirely

---

#### 4. The Five Model Variants

##### Model 1 — FULL MODEL (baseline reference, symmetric params)
All terms present. This is the complete model from chineseOrgans.tex eq. (Section 3).

    ẋ_i = a·x_i - k·x_i² + c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i) - g·x_i/(1+x_{κ(i)}) - h·x_i·x_{ρ(i)} - b·x_{κ(i)}/(1+ε·x_i)

Default parameters: a=2.0, k=0.5, c=1.0, g=0.5, h=0.3, b=0.2, ε=0.1

##### Model 2 — ADAPTIVE SET POINT
The self-regulation term becomes adaptive: the set point θ_i depends on the organ's
Sheng mother (nourishment raises it) and Ke controller (suppression lowers it).
The logistic term `a·x_i - k·x_i²` is replaced by `k·x_i·(θ₀ + α·x_{σ(i)} - β·x_{ρ(i)} - x_i)`,
which expands to include coupling through the set point dynamics.

##### Model 3 — NO BACK-DRAW (ignore backtrack of usage of element)
The Sheng nourishment term loses its saturation denominator. The child's
weakness no longer reflexively increases the mother's push. The term simply
becomes a constant-coefficient input: `+c_lin·x_{σ(i)}` (or `+(c/10)·x_{σ(i)}`
to keep magnitudes comparable). The quadratic `k·x_i²` self-limitation remains.

##### Model 4 — NO WU REBELLIOUS TERM
Remove all `-b·x_{κ(i)}/(1+ε·x_i)` terms. Only normal Ke suppression (h terms)
and Ke over-acting (g terms) remain. This tests whether rebellious
counter-acting is necessary for the model's qualitative behavior.

##### Model 5 — TWO-NODE (decoupled motif analysis)
Reduce to just 2 nodes for local motif analysis. Two choices are natural:
  (A) Mother-Child pair: x1 (Wood/Liver) and x2 (Fire/Heart), Sheng x1→x2,
      with the Ke restraint x2 receives from x5, and x1 from x4 (can be set to constant or zero)
  (B) Ke pair: x1 (Wood) and x3 (Earth), where x1 controls x3 and x3 is controlled by x1

For XPP-AUT bifurcation/phase-plane analysis of just these 2 coupled nodes,
all other x_j are treated as fixed external parameters (p2, p3, etc.).

---

#### 5. Parameter Table (Symmetric Case)

| Symbol | Meaning | Default |
|--------|---------|---------|
| a | Self-production rate | 2.0 |
| k | Self-limitation (carrying capacity = a/k) | 0.5 |
| c | Sheng nourishment strength | 1.0 |
| c_lin | Sheng, no-backtrack version | 0.1 (= c/10) |
| g | Ke over-acting drain strength | 0.5 |
| h | Ke direct restraint strength | 0.3 |
| b | Wu rebellious counter-acting strength | 0.2 |
| eps | Wu threshold sensitivity | 0.1 |
| th0 | Baseline adaptive set point | 4.0 |
| alp | Adaptive set point mother coupling | 0.2 |
| bet | Adaptive set point controller coupling | 0.1 |

---

#### 6. XPP-AUT File Notes

- XPP uses `x1'=...` for dx1/dt
- Parameters declared with `par`
- Initial conditions with `init`
- `@` lines set solver options (method, dt, total time, bounds)
- For AUTO continuation: set up the parameter to continue in with `@ autovar`
  or launch AUTO from within XPPAUT GUI
- All 5 files are provided as standalone `.ode` files ready to load in XPPAUT

---

#### 7. TCM Interpretation of Each Model Variant

| Model | What it tests | TCM meaning |
|-------|--------------|-------------|
| Full | Complete dynamics | All physiological and pathological couplings |
| Adaptive | Set-point regulation | Organ health level adapts to its network neighborhood |
| No back-draw | Weak mother-child coupling | Child weakness does NOT reflexively boost mother output |
| No Wu | Only normal Ke suppression | No pathological rebellion; system stays in "healthy" regime |
| 2-node | Local motif dynamics | Isolated mother-child or controller-target pair |


## 1.2 The `.ode` files, verbatim

Each file is a self-contained XPPAUT model. Conventions: `par` lines declare parameters, `x1' = …` lines are the ODEs, `init` gives initial conditions, and lines starting with `@` set numerical/plot options. The two-node files freeze the three non-dynamic organs as parameters (`p3, p4, p5`), so a 2-D phase plane and AUTO continuation in those "external inputs" are possible.


### `wuxing_full.ode`

Full model (baseline reference): saturating Sheng, Ke over-acting, Ke restraint, Wu rebellion

```text
# wuxing_full.ode
# Wu-Xing Five-Organ Full Model
#
# State variables:
#   x1 = Wood/Liver,  x2 = Fire/Heart,  x3 = Earth/Spleen
#   x4 = Metal/Lung,  x5 = Water/Kidney
#
# Sheng (mother) cycle:  x5->x1->x2->x3->x4->x5
# Ke (control) cycle:    x4-|x1, x5-|x2, x1-|x3, x2-|x4, x3-|x5
#
# Each equation:
#   logistic self-reg  +  Sheng nourishment (with back-draw saturation)
#   - Ke over-acting   -  Ke direct restraint  -  Wu rebellious counter-acting
#
# Wu rebellious: when x_i is deficient, its Ke TARGET x_{kappa(i)} rebels
# against x_i, term: -b * x_{kappa(i)} / (1 + eps*x_i)
#   kappa(1)=3, kappa(2)=4, kappa(3)=5, kappa(4)=1, kappa(5)=2

# Parameters
par a=2.0, k=0.5
par c=1.0
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
par b=0.2
par eps=0.1

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver):  mother=x5, Ke-target=x3, Ke-controller=x4
x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*x5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+x3) - h*x1*x4 - b*x3/(1+eps*x1)

# x2 (Fire/Heart):  mother=x1, Ke-target=x4, Ke-controller=x5
x2' = a*x2 - k*x2^2 + c*x1/(1+x2) - g*x2/(1+x4) - h*x2*x5 - b*x4/(1+eps*x2)

# x3 (Earth/Spleen): mother=x2, Ke-target=x5, Ke-controller=x1
x3' = a*x3 - k*x3^2 + c*x2/(1+x3) - g*x3/(1+x5) - h*x3*x1 - b*x5/(1+eps*x3)

# x4 (Metal/Lung):  mother=x3, Ke-target=x1, Ke-controller=x2
x4' = a*x4 - k*x4^2 + c*x3/(1+x4) - g*x4/(1+x1) - h*x4*x2 - b*x1/(1+eps*x4)

# x5 (Water/Kidney): mother=x4, Ke-target=x2, Ke-controller=x3
x5' = a*x5 - k*x5^2 + c*x4/(1+x5) - g*x5/(1+x2) - h*x5*x3 - b*x2/(1+eps*x5)

# Initial conditions (symmetric healthy baseline ~ a/k = 4)
init x1=3.5, x2=3.5, x3=3.5, x4=3.5, x5=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ xlo=0, xhi=200, ylo=0, yhi=8
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

done
```


### `wuxing_adaptive.ode`

Adaptive set point: self-regulation `k x_i (θ_i − x_i)` with θ_i raised by the mother and lowered by the controller

```text
# wuxing_adaptive.ode
# Wu-Xing Five-Organ Model — ADAPTIVE SET POINT
#
# The self-regulation term replaces a*x_i - k*x_i^2 with:
#
#   k * x_i * (theta_i - x_i)
#
# where the set point theta_i is NOT fixed but adapts:
#
#   theta_i = th0 + alp * x_{sigma(i)}  -  bet * x_{rho(i)}
#
#   th0  = baseline constitutional set point
#   alp  = how strongly Sheng mother raises the set point
#   bet  = how strongly Ke controller lowers the set point
#
# Expanding:  k*x_i*(th0 + alp*x_mother - bet*x_ctrl - x_i)
#           = k*th0*x_i + k*alp*x_mother*x_i - k*bet*x_ctrl*x_i - k*x_i^2
#
# The effective "a" = k*th0 is now an emergent quantity from the set point.
# The set-point coupling adds extra x_i*x_mother and x_i*x_ctrl terms
# on top of the Sheng/Ke saturation terms.
#
# All other terms (Sheng nourishment, Ke over-acting, Ke restraint, Wu rebellious)
# are the same as the full model.
#
# Sheng mothers: sigma(1)=5, sigma(2)=1, sigma(3)=2, sigma(4)=3, sigma(5)=4
# Ke controllers: rho(1)=4, rho(2)=5, rho(3)=1, rho(4)=2, rho(5)=3
# Ke targets:     kappa(1)=3, kappa(2)=4, kappa(3)=5, kappa(4)=1, kappa(5)=2

# Parameters
par k=0.5
par th0=4.0
par alp=0.2
par bet=0.1
par c=1.0
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
par b=0.2
par eps=0.1

# Adaptive set points as auxiliary variables (for plotting/inspection)
aux theta1 = th0 + alp*x5 - bet*x4
aux theta2 = th0 + alp*x1 - bet*x5
aux theta3 = th0 + alp*x2 - bet*x1
aux theta4 = th0 + alp*x3 - bet*x2
aux theta5 = th0 + alp*x4 - bet*x3

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver):  mother=x5, ctrl=x4, Ke-target=x3
x1' = k*x1*(th0 + alp*x5 - bet*x4 - x1) \
      + c*x5/(1+x1) \
      - g*x1/(1+x3) \
      - h*x1*x4 \
      - b*x3/(1+eps*x1)

# x2 (Fire/Heart):  mother=x1, ctrl=x5, Ke-target=x4
x2' = k*x2*(th0 + alp*x1 - bet*x5 - x2) \
      + c*x1/(1+x2) \
      - g*x2/(1+x4) \
      - h*x2*x5 \
      - b*x4/(1+eps*x2)

# x3 (Earth/Spleen): mother=x2, ctrl=x1, Ke-target=x5
x3' = k*x3*(th0 + alp*x2 - bet*x1 - x3) \
      + c*x2/(1+x3) \
      - g*x3/(1+x5) \
      - h*x3*x1 \
      - b*x5/(1+eps*x3)

# x4 (Metal/Lung):  mother=x3, ctrl=x2, Ke-target=x1
x4' = k*x4*(th0 + alp*x3 - bet*x2 - x4) \
      + c*x3/(1+x4) \
      - g*x4/(1+x1) \
      - h*x4*x2 \
      - b*x1/(1+eps*x4)

# x5 (Water/Kidney): mother=x4, ctrl=x3, Ke-target=x2
x5' = k*x5*(th0 + alp*x4 - bet*x3 - x5) \
      + c*x4/(1+x5) \
      - g*x5/(1+x2) \
      - h*x5*x3 \
      - b*x2/(1+eps*x5)

# Initial conditions (near the natural baseline th0)
init x1=3.5, x2=3.5, x3=3.5, x4=3.5, x5=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ xlo=0, xhi=200, ylo=0, yhi=10
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

done
```


### `wuxing_no_backtrack.ode`

No back-draw: the Sheng term loses its `1/(1+x_i)` saturation and becomes linear (`c_lin = c/10`)

```text
# wuxing_no_backtrack.ode
# Wu-Xing Five-Organ Model — NO BACK-DRAW (No backtrack of element usage)
#
# In the full model, the Sheng nourishment term is:
#
#   + c * x_{sigma(i)} / (1 + x_i)
#
# The denominator (1 + x_i) is the "back-draw" or "child demand" saturation:
# when x_i is low (child is weak/deficient), 1/(1+x_i) → 1, so nourishment
# is HIGH — meaning the child's weakness reflexively increases the mother's
# output rate (the child "draws more" from the mother when deficient).
#
# In this model we REMOVE that saturation:
# The Sheng nourishment becomes simply a linear input:
#
#   + c_lin * x_{sigma(i)}
#
# where c_lin = c/10 to keep the magnitudes comparable
# (since the saturation denominator was ~1 near equilibrium,
# but the removal can over-amplify the term otherwise).
#
# Self-regulation (a*x_i - k*x_i^2) is UNCHANGED — the quadratic damping
# still provides carrying-capacity limitation; the removal only affects
# HOW the mother nourishes the child, not how each organ regulates itself.
#
# All Ke restraint, Ke over-acting, and Wu rebellious terms are unchanged.
#
# Ke targets:     kappa(1)=3, kappa(2)=4, kappa(3)=5, kappa(4)=1, kappa(5)=2
# Ke controllers: rho(1)=4, rho(2)=5, rho(3)=1, rho(4)=2, rho(5)=3
# Sheng mothers:  sigma(1)=5, sigma(2)=1, sigma(3)=2, sigma(4)=3, sigma(5)=4

# Parameters
par a=2.0, k=0.5
# c_lin is the no-backtrack Sheng coefficient (= c/10 by default)
par c_lin=0.1
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
par b=0.2
par eps=0.1

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver):  mother=x5, Ke-target=x3, Ke-controller=x4
x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c_lin*x5 - g*x1/(1+x3) - h*x1*x4 - b*x3/(1+eps*x1)

# x2 (Fire/Heart):  mother=x1, Ke-target=x4, Ke-controller=x5
x2' = a*x2 - k*x2^2 + c_lin*x1 - g*x2/(1+x4) - h*x2*x5 - b*x4/(1+eps*x2)

# x3 (Earth/Spleen): mother=x2, Ke-target=x5, Ke-controller=x1
x3' = a*x3 - k*x3^2 + c_lin*x2 - g*x3/(1+x5) - h*x3*x1 - b*x5/(1+eps*x3)

# x4 (Metal/Lung):  mother=x3, Ke-target=x1, Ke-controller=x2
x4' = a*x4 - k*x4^2 + c_lin*x3 - g*x4/(1+x1) - h*x4*x2 - b*x1/(1+eps*x4)

# x5 (Water/Kidney): mother=x4, Ke-target=x2, Ke-controller=x3
x5' = a*x5 - k*x5^2 + c_lin*x4 - g*x5/(1+x2) - h*x5*x3 - b*x2/(1+eps*x5)

# Initial conditions
init x1=3.5, x2=3.5, x3=3.5, x4=3.5, x5=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ xlo=0, xhi=200, ylo=0, yhi=8
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

done
```


### `wuxing_no_wu.ode`

No Wu: the full model without the rebellion term

```text
# wuxing_no_wu.ode
# Wu-Xing Five-Organ Model — NO WU (REBELLIOUS COUNTER-ACTING) TERM
#
# The full model includes a Wu (侮, rebellious counter-acting) term:
#
#   - b * x_{kappa(i)} / (1 + eps * x_i)
#
# This represents the pathological situation where when x_i is severely
# deficient (x_i small → 1/(1+eps*x_i) → 1), the organ x_i normally
# controls (its Ke target x_{kappa(i)}) REBELS BACK against x_i and
# drains it further. This is 相侮 (Xiang Wu).
#
# In this model, ALL Wu rebellious terms are REMOVED.
# Only the standard Ke relations remain:
#   - Ke over-acting: -g*x_i/(1+x_{kappa(i)})
#   - Ke direct restraint: -h*x_i*x_{rho(i)}
#
# This models the "healthy regime" where pathological counter-acting
# never arises. Comparing with the full model reveals what dynamical
# role Wu rebellious counter-acting plays.
#
# Ke targets:     kappa(1)=3, kappa(2)=4, kappa(3)=5, kappa(4)=1, kappa(5)=2
# Ke controllers: rho(1)=4, rho(2)=5, rho(3)=1, rho(4)=2, rho(5)=3
# Sheng mothers:  sigma(1)=5, sigma(2)=1, sigma(3)=2, sigma(4)=3, sigma(5)=4

# Parameters
par a=2.0, k=0.5
par c=1.0
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
# Note: b and eps are NOT used in this model (Wu terms removed)

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver):  mother=x5, Ke-target=x3, Ke-controller=x4
x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*x5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+x3) - h*x1*x4

# x2 (Fire/Heart):  mother=x1, Ke-target=x4, Ke-controller=x5
x2' = a*x2 - k*x2^2 + c*x1/(1+x2) - g*x2/(1+x4) - h*x2*x5

# x3 (Earth/Spleen): mother=x2, Ke-target=x5, Ke-controller=x1
x3' = a*x3 - k*x3^2 + c*x2/(1+x3) - g*x3/(1+x5) - h*x3*x1

# x4 (Metal/Lung):  mother=x3, Ke-target=x1, Ke-controller=x2
x4' = a*x4 - k*x4^2 + c*x3/(1+x4) - g*x4/(1+x1) - h*x4*x2

# x5 (Water/Kidney): mother=x4, Ke-target=x2, Ke-controller=x3
x5' = a*x5 - k*x5^2 + c*x4/(1+x5) - g*x5/(1+x2) - h*x5*x3

# Initial conditions
init x1=3.5, x2=3.5, x3=3.5, x4=3.5, x5=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ xlo=0, xhi=200, ylo=0, yhi=8
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

done
```


### `wuxing_2node_sheng.ode`

Two-node Sheng motif: Wood → Fire dynamic, other organs frozen

```text
# wuxing_2node_sheng.ode
# Wu-Xing Two-Node Model — SHENG (MOTHER-CHILD) MOTIF
# Decoupled from the full 5-node system for local motif analysis
#
# Nodes: x1 (Wood/Liver) and x2 (Fire/Heart)
# Relationship: x1 is the Sheng MOTHER of x2  (x1 → x2)
#
# All other organs (x3, x4, x5) are treated as FIXED EXTERNAL PARAMETERS:
#   p3 = fixed level of x3 (Earth/Spleen)
#   p4 = fixed level of x4 (Metal/Lung)
#   p5 = fixed level of x5 (Water/Kidney)
#
# This allows 2D phase-plane analysis, nullcline plotting, and AUTO
# bifurcation continuation as p3, p4, p5 are varied as external inputs.
#
# Full equations (from the full model) restricted to x1, x2:
#
# x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*p5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+p3) - h*x1*p4 - b*p3/(1+eps*x1)
#       [x1's mother is x5=p5; Ke-target is x3=p3; Ke-ctrl is x4=p4; Wu-rebel from x3=p3]
#
# x2' = a*x2 - k*x2^2 + c*x1/(1+x2) - g*x2/(1+p4) - h*x2*p5 - b*p4/(1+eps*x2)
#       [x2's mother is x1 (active!); Ke-target is x4=p4; Ke-ctrl is x5=p5; Wu-rebel from x4=p4]
#
# The KEY COUPLING between x1 and x2 is the Sheng term in x2's equation:
#   + c*x1/(1+x2)
# which represents x1 nourishing x2. All other interactions with the
# remaining 3 organs are frozen via the external parameters p3, p4, p5.

# Active state variables
# (only x1 and x2 are dynamic)

# Parameters — intrinsic
par a=2.0, k=0.5
par c=1.0
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
par b=0.2
par eps=0.1

# External frozen organ levels (bifurcation parameters for AUTO)
par p3=3.5
par p4=3.5
par p5=3.5

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver): mother=p5(fixed), Ke-target=p3(fixed), Ke-ctrl=p4(fixed)
x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*p5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+p3) - h*x1*p4 - b*p3/(1+eps*x1)

# x2 (Fire/Heart): mother=x1 (ACTIVE), Ke-target=p4(fixed), Ke-ctrl=p5(fixed)
x2' = a*x2 - k*x2^2 + c*x1/(1+x2) - g*x2/(1+p4) - h*x2*p5 - b*p4/(1+eps*x2)

# Initial conditions
init x1=3.5, x2=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ xlo=0, xhi=200, ylo=0, yhi=8
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

# For phase plane: set xp=x1, yp=x2
@ xp=x1, yp=x2
@ xlo=0, xhi=8, ylo=0, yhi=8

done
```


### `wuxing_2node_ke.ode`

Two-node Ke motif: Wood ⊣ Earth dynamic, other organs frozen

```text
# wuxing_2node_ke.ode
# Wu-Xing Two-Node Model — KE (CONTROLLER-TARGET) MOTIF
# Decoupled from the full 5-node system for local motif analysis
#
# Nodes: x1 (Wood/Liver) and x3 (Earth/Spleen)
# Relationship: x1 KE-CONTROLS x3  (x1 --|> x3)
#   i.e., x1 is the Ke controller of x3, and x3 is the Ke target of x1
#
# All other organs (x2, x4, x5) are treated as FIXED EXTERNAL PARAMETERS:
#   p2 = fixed level of x2 (Fire/Heart)
#   p4 = fixed level of x4 (Metal/Lung)
#   p5 = fixed level of x5 (Water/Kidney)
#
# Full equations restricted to x1, x3:
#
# x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*p5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+x3) - h*x1*p4 - b*x3/(1+eps*x1)
#       [x1: mother=p5; Ke-target=x3 (ACTIVE); Ke-ctrl=p4; Wu-rebel from x3 (ACTIVE)]
#       Note: x1/(1+x3) is the Ke over-acting: x1 drains when x3 is low
#             x3/(1+eps*x1) is Wu: x3 rebels against x1 when x1 is weak
#
# x3' = a*x3 - k*x3^2 + c*p2/(1+x3) - g*x3/(1+p5) - h*x3*x1 - b*p5/(1+eps*x3)
#       [x3: mother=p2; Ke-target=p5; Ke-ctrl=x1 (ACTIVE); Wu-rebel from p5]
#       Note: h*x3*x1 is the direct Ke restraint of x3 by x1
#
# The KEY COUPLINGS between x1 and x3 are:
#   In x1: -g*x1/(1+x3)   [Ke over-acting: x1 is drained when x3 is low]
#           -b*x3/(1+eps*x1) [Wu: x3 rebels against x1 when x1 is deficient]
#   In x3: -h*x3*x1        [Ke direct restraint: x1 suppresses x3]

# Parameters — intrinsic
par a=2.0, k=0.5
par c=1.0
par g=0.5
par h=0.3
par b=0.2
par eps=0.1

# External frozen organ levels (can be continued in AUTO)
par p2=3.5
par p4=3.5
par p5=3.5

# Equations
# x1 (Wood/Liver): mother=p5(fixed), Ke-target=x3 (ACTIVE), Ke-ctrl=p4(fixed)
x1' = a*x1 - k*x1^2 + c*p5/(1+x1) - g*x1/(1+x3) - h*x1*p4 - b*x3/(1+eps*x1)

# x3 (Earth/Spleen): mother=p2(fixed), Ke-target=p5(fixed), Ke-ctrl=x1 (ACTIVE)
x3' = a*x3 - k*x3^2 + c*p2/(1+x3) - g*x3/(1+p5) - h*x3*x1 - b*p5/(1+eps*x3)

# Initial conditions
init x1=3.5, x3=3.5

# Solver settings
@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200
@ maxstor=500000
@ bounds=1e6

# For phase plane: set xp=x1, yp=x3
@ xp=x1, yp=x3
@ xlo=0, xhi=8, ylo=0, yhi=8

done
```


## 1.3 The XPPAUT / AUTO questions that followed

These were short troubleshooting exchanges (several with screenshots). The substance of the answers:

* **"Why does the time series go all the way to 200, how do I change it?"** The two option lines
  `@ method=rk4, dt=0.05, total=200` and `@ xlo=0, xhi=200, …` set (a) how long XPPAUT integrates (`total`) and (b) how wide the plot window is (`xhi`). Change both together — e.g. `total=50` and `xhi=50` — or do it in the GUI via *nUmerics → Total* and *Window/zoom*. Editing only `xhi` merely crops the picture.
* **"Can I just delete the solver settings?"** Yes. `@` lines only override XPPAUT's defaults (total 20, dt 0.05, Runge–Kutta, 5000 stored points, bounds 100). The reason they were written in is that a long run with dt=0.05 stores more than the default 5000 points (hence `maxstor`), and that the default `bounds` would abort a run if any variable exceeded 100. Our model is bounded, so deleting them only changes defaults, not the equations.
* **"Are all the variants supposed to be the same?"** No. They are different *hypotheses* about the physiology (adaptive set point, no back-draw, no rebellion, two-node motifs). The point is to ask which structural ingredient creates or destroys which bifurcation — so they should differ.
* **"Is the point to compare them, or to do bifurcation analysis?"** Both: the variants are the *models*; bifurcation analysis (AUTO) is the *tool* applied to each model, tracking steady states as one parameter changes. You also pasted an older AUTO Fortran file for a simple homeostasis model; it plays the same role for that earlier model.
* **"How do I find bifurcations in XPPAUT?"** (1) Run the model from an initial condition until it settles. (2) *Sing pts → Go* to get the exact equilibrium (your screenshot showed X1…X5 = 2.4793, STABLE). (3) *File → Auto*; in the AUTO window choose *Parameter* (the bifurcation parameter, e.g. `g`), *Axes* (x1 vs that parameter), set the numerical ranges, then *Run → Steady state*. (4) Use *Grab* to pick a special point (LP/HB) and continue it in two parameters for a stability boundary.
* **"The equilibrium values changed."** Expected: the equilibrium depends on the parameters, so after changing a parameter and re-running *Sing pts*, a different x\* is correct (it is the point on the branch you are now sitting at).

*(The screenshots are not reproduced here; this section records the guidance, not the images.)*


# Part 2 — Literature, relations, motifs

**Your requests:** (a) a list of all the relationships that need to be modelled in Wu-Xing (using your `main2.tex` and `chineseOrgans.tex`); (b) search the literature on modelling Wu-Xing and design a model with every relevant factor — explain why each part is there and whether to keep or change your LaTeX; (c) since you will write about network motifs, explain motifs and list what is relevant; (d) explain how to use bifurcation analysis and what to look for, because the earlier model's plots "didn't show anything interesting".

The relations that became model terms:

| Relation | Classical name | Term in the saturating model |
|---|---|---|
| organ's own generation / saturation | 自 | `a x_i − k x_i²` |
| mother nourishes child | 生 (Sheng) | `+ c x_{i−1}/(1+x_i)` |
| child draws on / exhausts mother | 子盗母气 | demand term `g_d x_{i+1}x_i/(1+x_i)` (extended model) |
| controller restrains organ | 克 (Ke) | `− h x_i x_{i−2}` |
| organ restrains / over-acts on its target | 相乘 | `− g x_i/(1+x_{i+2})` |
| weakened organ is counter-attacked | 相侮 | `− b x_{i+2}/(1+ε x_i)` |
| mother's disease reaches child | 母病及子 | emerges from the Sheng chain |

The complete written answer (literature review, relation-by-relation justification, the recommended model, the network-motif section checklist, and the bifurcation recipe) is reproduced in full in **Appendix C**.


# Part 3 — Lin & Han (2020): the maths and the Python reproduction

**Your request:** reproduce and fully understand Lin & Han, *Analysis of Dynamic Five-Element Model* (Proc. 39th Chinese Control Conference, 2020): the pure mathematics and the test diagrams; look back at the literature; apply the same process to the modified model; compare network motifs; connect to other fields (ergodicity); and "give me python code to reproduce and all steps".

### 3.1 What the paper does, in plain terms

Lin studies the bilinear system of §0.2 and reports five kinds of result:

1. **Case 1, all coefficients 1.** The balanced state x\* = 1/3 exists, but its Jacobian has eigenvalues −1, −0.539 ± 0.392i, **+0.206 ± 0.634i**, so it is **unstable**. Trajectories settle onto a **periodic orbit** (period T ≈ 17.626).
2. **Restraint only (d = h = 1, others 0), Fig 2.** Pure Ke dynamics: at least three organs decay to zero while the remaining two settle at positive values.
3. **Generation only, c = g = 1 (others 0), Figs 4–5.** An irregular but bounded trajectory; the divergence of the vector field is (c − g)Σx = 0, so phase-space volume is conserved. Lin calls it chaotic, with Lyapunov exponents summing to zero.
4. **Ergodicity-flavoured arguments** about the conserved quantities.
5. **Lyapunov spectra** as the evidence for "periodic" (largest exponent ≈ 0) versus "chaotic" (largest exponent > 0).

The full line-by-line explanation of the paper is reproduced in **Appendix D**.

### 3.2 `lin_reproduce.py` — what the code does

* `rhs` is the right-hand side of the bilinear system written with explicit loops, so every neighbour is visible: `mother = x[(i−1)%5]`, `controller = x[(i−2)%5]`, `child = x[(i+1)%5]`, `target = x[(i+2)%5]`.
* `jacobian` is the **analytic** Jacobian: the diagonal entry is the derivative of row *i* with respect to x_i, and each coupling adds one off-diagonal entry in the column of the neighbour it involves.
* `integrate` calls SciPy's `solve_ivp` (RK45, rtol 1e-9, atol 1e-12, `max_step = 5·dt`) and returns the dense time grid.
* `case1_equilibrium_stability` — sets all coefficients to 1, computes x\* = −a/(c−b−d−g−h) = 1/3, builds the Jacobian at x\*·1, and prints its eigenvalues next to the paper's.
* `fig2_RisR`, `fig3_periodic`, `fig45_chaos` — reproduce the paper's Figures 2–5 (details below).
* `largest_lyapunov` — the **crude two-trajectory (Benettin) estimator**. It is kept here as the *first attempt*, and its result is **not trustworthy** (see §3.4); a proper variational/QR method replaced it.

*lin_reproduce.py (verbatim)*

```python
"""
Reproduction of Lin & Han (2020), "Analysis of Dynamic Five-Element Model",
Proc. 39th Chinese Control Conference.

Symmetric system (their eq. 2), 0-indexed x[0..4] = elements 1..5:

  dx_i/dt = a*x_i - b*x_i^2                      (gain, saturation)
          + c*x_i*x_{i-1}                        (isG: generated by mother)
          - d*x_i*x_{i-2}                        (isR: restricted by controller)
          - g*x_i*x_{i+1}                        (G : cost of generating child)
          - h*x_i*x_{i+2}                        (R : cost of restricting target)

Index maps (0-indexed, mod 5):
  mother     = i-1     (+c)
  controller = i-2     (-d)
  child      = i+1     (-g)
  target     = i+2     (-h)
"""

import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import matplotlib
matplotlib.use("Agg")
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Element display names in the paper's cyclic order (Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, Water)
NAMES = ["Wood", "Fire", "Earth", "Metal", "Water"]
COLORS = ["green", "red", "magenta", "black", "blue"]  # match paper's legend-ish


def rhs(t, x, a, b, c, d, g, h):
    x = np.asarray(x)
    f = np.empty(5)
    for i in range(5):
        mother     = x[(i - 1) % 5]
        controller = x[(i - 2) % 5]
        child      = x[(i + 1) % 5]
        target     = x[(i + 2) % 5]
        f[i] = (a * x[i] - b * x[i]**2
                + c * x[i] * mother
                - d * x[i] * controller
                - g * x[i] * child
                - h * x[i] * target)
    return f


def jacobian(x, a, b, c, d, g, h):
    """Analytic Jacobian of the symmetric system."""
    J = np.zeros((5, 5))
    for i in range(5):
        im1 = (i - 1) % 5   # mother (+c)
        im2 = (i - 2) % 5   # controller (-d)
        ip1 = (i + 1) % 5   # child (-g)
        ip2 = (i + 2) % 5   # target (-h)
        # diagonal
        J[i, i] = (a - 2 * b * x[i]
                   + c * x[im1] - d * x[im2] - g * x[ip1] - h * x[ip2])
        # off-diagonals from the cross terms
        J[i, im1] += c * x[i]
        J[i, im2] += -d * x[i]
        J[i, ip1] += -g * x[i]
        J[i, ip2] += -h * x[i]
    return J


def integrate(params, x0, t_end, dt=0.001, method="RK45"):
    a, b, c, d, g, h = params
    t_eval = np.arange(0, t_end, dt)
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0, t_end), x0, args=params,
                    t_eval=t_eval, method=method, rtol=1e-9, atol=1e-12,
                    max_step=dt*5)
    return sol.t, sol.y


# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
# CASE 1: a=b=c=d=g=h=1, symmetric equilibrium (1/3,...) stability
# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
def case1_equilibrium_stability():
    a=b=c=d=g=h = 1.0
    # symmetric equilibrium x*: x*(a + (c-b-d-g-h)x*) = 0
    coeff = (c - b - d - g - h)          # = 1-1-1-1-1 = -3
    xstar = -a / coeff                    # = 1/3
    xeq = np.full(5, xstar)
    J = jacobian(xeq, a,b,c,d,g,h)
    eig = np.linalg.eigvals(J)
    print("=== CASE 1: a=b=c=d=g=h=1 ===")
    print(f"symmetric equilibrium x* = {xstar:.6f}  (paper: 1/3 = {1/3:.6f})")
    print("Jacobian:\n", J)
    print("eigenvalues:")
    for e in sorted(eig, key=lambda z: z.real):
        print(f"   {e.real:+.4f} {e.imag:+.4f}i")
    print("paper eigenvalues: -1, 0.206+/-0.634i, -0.539+/-0.392i")
    print("max real part =", eig.real.max(), "-> unstable\n")
    return eig


# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
# CASE 3 / Fig 2: R&isR only  (d=h=1, a=b=c=g=0), IC=(1,2,3,4,5)
# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
def fig2_RisR():
    params = (0,0,0,1,0,1)  # a,b,c,d,g,h  -> d=1,h=1
    x0 = [1,2,3,4,5]
    t, y = integrate(params, x0, t_end=6, dt=0.001)
    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6,4))
    for i in range(5):
        ax.plot(t, y[i], color=COLORS[i], label=NAMES[i])
    ax.set_xlabel("time"); ax.set_ylabel(r"variable $x_i$")
    ax.set_title("Fig 2 reproduction: R&isR only (d=h=1)")
    ax.legend()
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("fig2_RisR.png", dpi=130)
    print("=== FIG 2 (R&isR) ===")
    print("final state:", np.round(y[:,-1],4))
    print("(paper: monotone decay, >=3 variables -> 0)\n")


# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
# Fig 3: periodic solution  a=b=c=d=g=h=1, IC=(1,2,3,4,5)
# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
def fig3_periodic():
    params = (1,1,1,1,1,1)
    x0 = [1,2,3,4,5]
    t, y = integrate(params, x0, t_end=300, dt=0.01)
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(2,1, figsize=(6,6))
    for i in range(5):
        axes[0].plot(t, y[i], color=COLORS[i], label=NAMES[i], lw=0.8)
    axes[0].set_xlabel("time"); axes[0].set_ylabel(r"$x_i$")
    axes[0].set_title("Fig 3 reproduction: all params = 1 (periodic)")
    axes[0].legend(fontsize=7, ncol=5)
    axes[1].plot(t, y[1], color="red")  # Fire = index 1
    axes[1].set_xlabel("time"); axes[1].set_ylabel(r"$x_2$ (Fire)")
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("fig3_periodic.png", dpi=130)

    # estimate period from Fire peaks in the settled portion
    fire = y[1]
    mask = t > 150
    tt, ff = t[mask], fire[mask]
    peaks = []
    for k in range(1, len(ff)-1):
        if ff[k] > ff[k-1] and ff[k] > ff[k+1] and ff[k] > np.mean(ff):
            peaks.append(tt[k])
    if len(peaks) > 2:
        period = np.mean(np.diff(peaks))
    else:
        period = float("nan")
    print("=== FIG 3 (periodic) ===")
    print(f"estimated period T = {period:.3f}  (paper: T ~ 17.626)\n")


# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
# Fig 4/5: chaotic (conservative) case  c=g=1, a=b=d=h=0, IC=(1,2,3,4,5)
# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
def fig45_chaos():
    params = (0,0,1,0,1,0)  # c=1, g=1
    x0 = [1,2,3,4,5]
    t, y = integrate(params, x0, t_end=120, dt=0.005)

    # time series
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(2,1, figsize=(6,6))
    for i in range(5):
        axes[0].plot(t, y[i], color=COLORS[i], label=NAMES[i], lw=0.6)
    axes[0].set_xlabel("time"); axes[0].set_ylabel(r"$x_i$")
    axes[0].set_title("Fig 4 reproduction: c=g=1 (chaotic, conservative)")
    axes[0].legend(fontsize=7, ncol=5)
    axes[1].plot(t, y[1], color="red", lw=0.6)
    axes[1].set_xlabel("time"); axes[1].set_ylabel(r"$x_2$ (Fire)")
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("fig4_chaos_timeseries.png", dpi=130)

    # phase diagrams: Fire (idx1) vs others
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(2,2, figsize=(7,7))
    pairs = [(2,1,"Earth"), (3,1,"Metal"), (4,1,"Water"), (0,1,"Wood")]
    for ax,(xi,yi,lab) in zip(axes.flat, pairs):
        ax.plot(y[xi], y[yi], color="blue", lw=0.3)
        ax.set_xlabel(f"x ({lab})"); ax.set_ylabel("x (Fire)")
    fig.suptitle("Fig 5 reproduction: phase diagrams, Fire vs others")
    fig.tight_layout(); fig.savefig("fig5_chaos_phase.png", dpi=130)
    print("=== FIG 4/5 (chaos) ===")
    print("range of Fire over run:", np.round(y[1].min(),3), "to", np.round(y[1].max(),3))
    print("(paper: irregular, bounded, no period)\n")
    return t, y


# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
# Largest Lyapunov exponent (Benettin method) for the chaos case
# ----------------------------------------------------------------------
def largest_lyapunov(params, x0, t_end=2000, dt=0.01, d0=1e-8):
    a,b,c,d,g,h = params
    x = np.array(x0, float)
    xp = x + d0*np.ones(5)/np.sqrt(5)
    n_steps = int(t_end/dt)
    s = 0.0
    from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
    for _ in range(n_steps):
        for state in (x, xp):
            sol = solve_ivp(rhs,(0,dt),state,args=params,method="RK45",
                            rtol=1e-9,atol=1e-12)
            state[:] = sol.y[:,-1]
        diff = xp - x
        dist = np.linalg.norm(diff)
        if dist == 0: break
        s += np.log(dist/d0)
        xp = x + (diff)*(d0/dist)   # renormalize
    return s/(n_steps*dt)


def volume_contraction_symbolic():
    """Divergence of the vector field = trace of Jacobian, integrated meaning.
       For the conservative chaos case c=g, div should sum to (c-g)*sum(x)=0."""
    print("=== phase-space volume (divergence) check ===")
    print("div F = sum_i dF_i/dx_i")
    print("For c=g, a=b=d=h=0: div F = (c-g)*sum(x_i) = 0  -> conservative")
    print("(matches paper: sum of Lyapunov exponents = 0)\n")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    case1_equilibrium_stability()
    fig2_RisR()
    fig3_periodic()
    fig45_chaos()
    volume_contraction_symbolic()

    print("Computing largest Lyapunov exponent for chaos case (may take ~1 min)...")
    lam = largest_lyapunov((0,0,1,0,1,0), [1,2,3,4,5], t_end=400, dt=0.01)
    print(f"largest Lyapunov exponent ~ {lam:.4f}  (paper: ~0.67, positive -> chaos)")
```

#### How the four figures are built

* **Fig 2 (restraint only).** Parameters `(a,b,c,d,g,h) = (0,0,0,1,0,1)`, initial condition (1,2,3,4,5), integrated to t = 6 on a grid of step 0.001. One line per organ (green = Wood, red = Fire, magenta = Earth, black = Metal, blue = Water). With only Ke terms every derivative is `−x_i(x_{i−2} + x_{i+2})`, which is ≤ 0, so the curves can only fall — three of them are driven to zero and the other two level off.
* **Fig 3 (periodic).** All coefficients 1, t up to 300 (step 0.01). Top panel: all five organs; bottom panel: Fire alone, so the oscillation is easy to see. The period is measured on the *settled* part (t > 150) as the mean spacing between local maxima of Fire that exceed Fire's mean.
* **Fig 4 (time series of the generation-only case).** `(0,0,1,0,1,0)`, t up to 120. Same two-panel layout. The irregular, never-repeating waveform is what Lin calls chaos.
* **Fig 5 (phase portraits).** Fire is plotted against each other organ (Earth, Metal, Water, Wood) in four small panels, with a thin line (`lw=0.3`) so that the trajectory's structure shows as a dense filled region rather than a single curve.

## 3.4 The Lyapunov exponents (and why there are two files)

**Your questions:** "why did you give me `lin_reproduce.py` *and* `lyapunov_spectrum.py` — how are they used together; how is `lin_reproduce` not enough; and explain the Lyapunov stuff."

* `lin_reproduce.py` reproduces the *pictures* (time series, phase plots) and the equilibrium numbers. Its Lyapunov routine is a quick two-trajectory estimate: run a reference orbit and a nearby copy, periodically measure their separation, renormalise to the original distance, and average the logs of the stretch. That gives only the **largest** exponent and is sensitive to step size and distance. In the run above it printed **0.0177**, against the paper's reported ≈ **0.67**.
* `lyapunov_spectrum.py` computes the **whole spectrum** by the standard method. Alongside the 5 state equations it integrates 5 *tangent vectors* with `dQ/dt = J(x(t))·Q` (25 extra variables), and every `dt` it re-orthonormalises with a QR decomposition; the average of `log|R_ii|` over time gives the five exponents. It needs the analytic Jacobian, which is why the Jacobian function is repeated inside it.

**What the exponents mean.** λ₁ is the average exponential rate at which a tiny error grows. λ₁ > 0 together with a bounded orbit = chaos; λ₁ = 0 = a limit cycle or other non-chaotic recurrent orbit; all λ < 0 = a stable fixed point. For a *continuous-time, bounded* orbit that is not an equilibrium, **one exponent must be exactly zero** (the direction along the flow: if x(t) is a solution then v(t) = F(x(t)) solves the variational equation, and it neither grows nor shrinks exponentially for a bounded orbit). The sum of all exponents equals the average divergence of F — zero for the conservative case c = g.

*lyapunov_spectrum.py (verbatim)*

```python
"""
Full Lyapunov spectrum for the Lin symmetric five-element system using the
standard variational (tangent-space) method with Gram-Schmidt reorthonormalization.

We integrate the 5 state equations together with 5 tangent vectors (25 extra
variables) and periodically QR-reorthonormalize, accumulating log stretch factors.
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp

def rhs(x, p):
    a,b,c,d,g,h = p
    f = np.empty(5)
    for i in range(5):
        mo=x[(i-1)%5]; co=x[(i-2)%5]; ch=x[(i+1)%5]; ta=x[(i+2)%5]
        f[i]= a*x[i]-b*x[i]**2 + c*x[i]*mo - d*x[i]*co - g*x[i]*ch - h*x[i]*ta
    return f

def jac(x, p):
    a,b,c,d,g,h = p
    J=np.zeros((5,5))
    for i in range(5):
        im1=(i-1)%5; im2=(i-2)%5; ip1=(i+1)%5; ip2=(i+2)%5
        J[i,i]=a-2*b*x[i]+c*x[im1]-d*x[im2]-g*x[ip1]-h*x[ip2]
        J[i,im1]+=c*x[i]; J[i,im2]+=-d*x[i]; J[i,ip1]+=-g*x[i]; J[i,ip2]+=-h*x[i]
    return J

def combined(t, X, p):
    x = X[:5]
    Q = X[5:].reshape(5,5)
    dx = rhs(x, p)
    J = jac(x, p)
    dQ = J @ Q
    return np.concatenate([dx, dQ.flatten()])

def lyapunov_spectrum(p, x0, t_end=1000, dt=0.5, transient=100):
    x = np.array(x0, float)
    # settle transient
    sol = solve_ivp(lambda t,y: rhs(y,p),(0,transient),x,method="RK45",
                    rtol=1e-10,atol=1e-12); x = sol.y[:,-1]
    Q = np.eye(5)
    lyap = np.zeros(5)
    n = int(t_end/dt)
    for _ in range(n):
        X0 = np.concatenate([x, Q.flatten()])
        sol = solve_ivp(combined,(0,dt),X0,args=(p,),method="RK45",
                        rtol=1e-10,atol=1e-12)
        Xe = sol.y[:,-1]
        x = Xe[:5]
        Qn = Xe[5:].reshape(5,5)
        Q, R = np.linalg.qr(Qn)
        lyap += np.log(np.abs(np.diag(R)))
    return np.sort(lyap/(n*dt))[::-1]

if __name__ == "__main__":
    print("Chaos case c=g=1, rest 0:")
    sp = lyapunov_spectrum((0,0,1,0,1,0),[1,2,3,4,5], t_end=1500, dt=0.5)
    print("  spectrum:", np.round(sp,4))
    print("  sum =", round(sp.sum(),4), "(paper: ~0, conservative)")
    print("  paper: 0.6700, 0.6606, -0.0761, -0.0794, -1.1751")
    print()
    print("Periodic case all=1:")
    sp2 = lyapunov_spectrum((1,1,1,1,1,1),[1,2,3,4,5], t_end=1500, dt=0.5)
    print("  spectrum:", np.round(sp2,4))
    print("  (largest should be ~0 for a limit cycle)")
```

**Fresh run of lyapunov_spectrum.py:**

```text
Chaos case c=g=1, rest 0:
  spectrum: [ 0.0049  0.0035 -0.0005 -0.0037 -0.0042]
  sum = 0.0 (paper: ~0, conservative)
  paper: 0.6700, 0.6606, -0.0761, -0.0794, -1.1751

Periodic case all=1:
  spectrum: [-0.0011 -0.1218 -0.2706 -0.2726 -1.0003]
  (largest should be ~0 for a limit cycle)
```

**Findings.**

* **Periodic case (all = 1):** spectrum ≈ (−0.0011, −0.1218, −0.2706, −0.2726, −1.0003). The leading exponent is ≈ 0 (the small negative value is finite-time error) — a **limit cycle**, as expected. The other four are negative, so the cycle is attracting.
* **c = g = 1 case:** spectrum ≈ (0.0049, 0.0035, −0.0005, −0.0037, −0.0042), **sum = 0** (volume-conserving, as the divergence argument says). All five are within 0.005 of zero. This is **not** the positive ≈ 0.67 reported in the paper, and the spectrum has no zero exponent isolated from two small positive ones; the values are consistent with finite-time noise around zero. **Our code does not reproduce a chaotic exponent for this case.** What the evidence supports: the orbit is bounded and irregular-looking, the sum is zero, and the leading exponent shrinks as the averaging time grows (next check). That is the pattern of a conservative system with (quasi-)periodic or very weakly mixing motion, not of a strongly chaotic one. Whether Lin's claim is right is therefore *unresolved by us*; the paper should say what was and was not reproduced.

I had not independently re-run those in the conversation, so I wrote `verify_lyapunov_claims.py` and ran it:

*verify_lyapunov_claims.py (re-created; full)*

```python
"""
Re-checks the statements made about Lin's Lyapunov spectra:
  (1) conserved quantities in the c=g=1 system,
  (2) eigenvalues of the Jacobian at the end state of the d=h=1 (R & isR) run,
  (3) finite-time largest exponent of the c=g=1 system at T = 500, 1000, 2000
      (the 1/T decay suggests a true value of zero),
  (4) the "flow-direction" test: a bounded non-equilibrium orbit must have one zero exponent,
      because v(t)=F(x(t)) solves the variational equation; so (1/t) log|F(x(t))| -> 0.
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp

def rhs(t,x,p):
    a,b,c,d,g,h=p
    f=np.empty(5)
    for i in range(5):
        mo=x[(i-1)%5]; co=x[(i-2)%5]; ch=x[(i+1)%5]; ta=x[(i+2)%5]
        f[i]=a*x[i]-b*x[i]**2+c*x[i]*mo-d*x[i]*co-g*x[i]*ch-h*x[i]*ta
    return f

def jac(x,p):
    a,b,c,d,g,h=p; J=np.zeros((5,5))
    for i in range(5):
        im1,im2,ip1,ip2=(i-1)%5,(i-2)%5,(i+1)%5,(i+2)%5
        J[i,i]=a-2*b*x[i]+c*x[im1]-d*x[im2]-g*x[ip1]-h*x[ip2]
        J[i,im1]+=c*x[i]; J[i,im2]+=-d*x[i]; J[i,ip1]+=-g*x[i]; J[i,ip2]+=-h*x[i]
    return J

def comb(t,X,p):
    x=X[:5]; Q=X[5:].reshape(5,5)
    return np.concatenate([rhs(0,x,p),(jac(x,p)@Q).ravel()])

CHAOS=(0,0,1,0,1,0); PERIODIC=(1,1,1,1,1,1); RISR=(0,0,0,1,0,1)
x0=[1,2,3,4,5]

print("(1) conserved quantities, c=g=1 (sum of x and product of x)")
s=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,500),x0,args=(CHAOS,),method="RK45",rtol=1e-12,atol=1e-14,
            t_eval=np.linspace(0,500,6))
for t,y in zip(s.t,s.y.T):
    print(f"   t={t:6.1f}  sum={y.sum():.12f}  prod={np.prod(y):.12f}")
print("   drift: sum", np.ptp(s.y.sum(0)), " prod", np.ptp(np.prod(s.y,axis=0)))

print("\n(2) d=h=1 run: Jacobian eigenvalues at the final state")
s=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,60),x0,args=(RISR,),method="RK45",rtol=1e-12,atol=1e-14)
xe=s.y[:,-1]; print("   final state:",np.round(xe,4))
print("   eigenvalues:",np.round(np.sort(np.linalg.eigvals(jac(xe,RISR)).real),4))

print("\n(3) finite-time largest exponent, c=g=1, QR/Benettin, transient 100 discarded")
p=CHAOS; dt=0.5
sol=solve_ivp(lambda t,y:rhs(t,y,p),(0,100),np.array(x0,float),rtol=1e-10,atol=1e-12)
x=sol.y[:,-1]; Q=np.eye(5); acc=np.zeros(5); T=0.0
checks={500,1000,2000}
while T<2000-1e-9:
    X=solve_ivp(comb,(0,dt),np.concatenate([x,Q.ravel()]),args=(p,),
                rtol=1e-10,atol=1e-12).y[:,-1]
    x=X[:5]; Q,R=np.linalg.qr(X[5:].reshape(5,5)); acc+=np.log(np.abs(np.diag(R))); T+=dt
    if round(T) in checks and abs(T-round(T))<1e-9:
        print(f"   T={int(round(T)):5d}  spectrum={np.round(np.sort(acc/T)[::-1],4)}  lambda_max={np.max(acc/T):.4f}")
        checks.discard(int(round(T)))

print("\n(4) flow-direction test: (1/t) log|F(x(t))| over t in [100, 2000]")
for name,pp in [("periodic (all=1)",PERIODIC),("conservative c=g=1",CHAOS)]:
    t=np.array([100.0,2000.0])
    s=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,2000),x0,args=(pp,),method="RK45",rtol=1e-10,atol=1e-12,t_eval=t)
    nF=[np.linalg.norm(rhs(0,s.y[:,i],pp)) for i in range(2)]
    print(f"   {name:22s} |F(100)|={nF[0]:.4f} |F(2000)|={nF[1]:.4f}  slope of log|F| = {(np.log(nF[1])-np.log(nF[0]))/1900:+.5f}")
```

**Output:**

```text
   t=   0.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000000000
   t= 100.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000010930
   t= 200.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000021925
   t= 300.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000032955
   t= 400.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000043842
   t= 500.0  sum=15.000000000000  prod=120.000000054858
   drift: sum 2.6645352591003757e-14  prod 5.4857977715983e-08

(2) d=h=1 run: Jacobian eigenvalues at the final state
   final state: [0.     0.     0.     2.3426 1.3038]
   eigenvalues: [-3.6464 -2.3426 -1.3038 -0.      0.    ]

(3) finite-time largest exponent, c=g=1, QR/Benettin, transient 100 discarded
   T=  500  spectrum=[ 0.0129  0.0084 -0.0021 -0.009  -0.0103]  lambda_max=0.0129
   T= 1000  spectrum=[ 0.0073  0.0046 -0.0009 -0.0052 -0.0059]  lambda_max=0.0073
   T= 2000  spectrum=[ 0.0039  0.0029 -0.0006 -0.003  -0.0032]  lambda_max=0.0039

(4) flow-direction test: (1/t) log|F(x(t))| over t in [100, 2000]
   periodic (all=1)       |F(100)|=0.2336 |F(2000)|=0.2904  slope of log|F| = +0.00011
   conservative c=g=1     |F(100)|=15.3005 |F(2000)|=20.3409  slope of log|F| = +0.00015
```

**Reading it.**

1. **Conserved quantities.** For c = g = 1 the sum Σx_i is conserved (drift ≈ 3×10⁻¹⁴) and so is the product Πx_i (relative drift ≈ 5×10⁻¹⁰ over 500 time units, the integrator's accumulated error). Both follow from the equations: Σẋ = Σx_i(x_{i−1} − x_{i+1}) = 0 and d/dt Σlog x_i = Σ(x_{i−1} − x_{i+1}) = 0. A 5-D flow with two conserved quantities is confined to a 3-D level set; a 3-D continuous flow can still be chaotic, so this alone does not settle the question.
2. **d = h = 1 end state.** At (0, 0, 0, 2.3426, 1.3038) the Jacobian has eigenvalues (0, 0, −1.3038, −2.3426, −3.6464): two zeros, three negative. The paper's reported spectrum for this case is (0.0015, 0.0006, −1.3159, −2.3517, −3.7092). The exact values are **−x₅, −x₄, −(x₄ + x₅)** — the paper's numbers are within about 2% of them, i.e. a *finite-time* version of the same fixed-point values. At an equilibrium all exponents must be real parts of Jacobian eigenvalues; here the two zero eigenvalues are structural (the two zero organs are at a degenerate equilibrium).
3. **Finite-time exponent for c = g = 1** (spectrum at T = 500 / 1000 / 2000). The leading value falls from 0.0129 (T = 500) to 0.0073 (T = 1000) to 0.0039 (T = 2000): it roughly halves each time T doubles, i.e. it falls like 1/T. A true positive exponent would level off at a constant. This 1/T decay is the signature of a true exponent equal to zero.
4. **Flow-direction test.** (1/t)·log‖F(x(t))‖ should tend to 0 for a bounded non-equilibrium orbit. It does for both cases (slopes of the order of 10⁻⁴), confirming the zero exponent associated with the flow direction. (The value quoted earlier in the conversation, −0.0006 for the periodic case, came from a slightly different window; the fresh value is the one above.)


# Part 4 — Colab notebook 1: regime classification and regime maps

**Your request:** "I was thinking of running code and finding something periodic … writing the graph and finding something that isn't chaotic and is also periodic — is that possible? And what are these files? Give me a full idea of what to run on Google Colab and what I can write about." I then asked you (with the interactive question tool) how to show results; you chose **"Both, side by side"** (Lin's model next to ours) and **"Both a map and picked example plots"**.

**What the notebook does.** It classifies the long-run behaviour of each model into four classes by integrating from the initial condition (1,2,3,4,5) and inspecting the last 40% of the run:

* **fixed** — every organ's range over the tail is < 10⁻³;
* **periodic** — the Fire time series has regularly spaced, equal-height peaks (coefficient of variation of both the peak spacing and the peak height < 0.06), or fewer than 4 peaks;
* **chaotic** — oscillating but not regular by that test;
* **blow-up** — non-finite values or values beyond 10³.

It then sweeps two parameters (Sheng strength *c* against Ke restraint *h*), colours each cell by class, and hunts for clean examples. A separate loop at the end scans along *h* and reports the largest Lyapunov exponent as a check that "periodic" really has λ₁ ≈ 0.

> **Two statements in this early notebook were superseded.** (a) It prints "Lin's model never has [a stable equilibrium]". That is true only at Lin's all-coefficients-1 baseline; over random coefficients about 23% of draws are stable (Part 7) and 45% under the full ordering (Part 8). The sentence has been corrected in the delivered notebook (the output printed below is from the run before that fix). (b) Its check labels c = g = 1 "chaotic (largest Lyapunov +0.028)" using the crude two-trajectory estimator of Part 3.4; the variational spectrum gives values within 0.013 of zero, so that label should not be taken as established.
>
> **And a caveat discovered later:** this notebook uses **one** initial condition. Section 9 (master notebook) repeats the classification from several initial conditions and shows that this matters a great deal for the bilinear model.

#### Wu-Xing Five-Element Dynamics: Lin vs Saturating Model
###### Sweep, classify (fixed / periodic / chaotic), map, and plot examples

This notebook:
1. Defines **both** models on the same five-element graph.
2. **Classifies** any parameter setting as fixed-point, periodic, or chaotic.
3. **Sweeps** two parameters and draws a **regime map** for each model, side by side.
4. **Auto-picks** clean examples from each regime and plots time series + phase portraits.

Run top to bottom (Runtime -> Run all). Nothing to upload.

**Cell 1 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Colab.ipynb`):**

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

NAMES  = ["Wood","Fire","Earth","Metal","Water"]
COLORS = ["green","red","magenta","black","blue"]
X0     = [1,2,3,4,5]          # standard off-equilibrium start (same as Lin)
```

##### 1. The two models

Both live on the **same graph** (mother = i-1, child = i+1, controller = i-2, target = i+2);
they differ only in the **functional form** of the couplings.

**Lin (bilinear / Lotka-Volterra):** every coupling is a mass-action product `x_i*x_j`.
Six influences: gain a, saturation b, isG c, isR d, G g, R h.

**Saturating (your model):** nourishment and over-acting saturate, which bounds the
trajectories and (as we'll see) can create a **stable healthy equilibrium** Lin's lacks.

**Cell 3 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Colab.ipynb`):**

```python
# Vectorized with np.roll:
#   np.roll(x, 1)=mother(i-1)  np.roll(x,2)=controller(i-2)
#   np.roll(x,-1)=child(i+1)   np.roll(x,-2)=target(i+2)

def rhs_lin(t, x, a,b,c,d,g,h):
    x=np.asarray(x)
    return (a*x - b*x**2
            + c*x*np.roll(x, 1)   # isG mother
            - d*x*np.roll(x, 2)   # isR controller
            - g*x*np.roll(x,-1)   # G child
            - h*x*np.roll(x,-2))  # R target

def rhs_sat(t, x, a,k,c,g,h):
    x=np.asarray(x)
    mother=np.roll(x,1); target=np.roll(x,-2); controller=np.roll(x,2)
    return (a*x - k*x**2
            + c*mother/(1+x)      # Sheng, demand-driven
            - g*x/(1+target)      # Ke over-acting
            - h*x*controller)     # Ke restraint
```

##### 2. Classifier

**Fast classifier (grid sweep):** integrate, drop transient, then — blowup? fixed
(tail barely moves)? else inspect Fire's peaks: regular spacing AND heights =>
**periodic**, wandering => **chaotic**. Cheap and separates Lin's limit cycle from its
conservative chaos.

**Lyapunov exponent (confirmation only):** >0 chaos, ~0 periodic, <0 fixed. Slower, so
not run per grid cell.

**Cell 5 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Colab.ipynb`):**

```python
def integrate(rhs, params, x0=X0, t_end=400, dt=0.05):
    return solve_ivp(rhs,(0,t_end),x0,args=params,method="RK45",
                     t_eval=np.arange(0,t_end,dt), rtol=1e-7, atol=1e-9)

def _peaks(v):
    return np.where((v[1:-1]>v[:-2]) & (v[1:-1]>v[2:]))[0] + 1

def classify(rhs, params, x0=X0, t_end=300, dt=0.1):
    sol=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,t_end),x0,args=params,method="RK45",
                  t_eval=np.arange(0,t_end,dt), rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8)
    if not sol.success: return "fail", sol
    y=sol.y; t=sol.t; tail=y[:, int(len(t)*0.6):]
    if (not np.all(np.isfinite(tail))) or np.max(np.abs(tail))>1e3:
        return "blowup", sol
    span=(tail.max(1)-tail.min(1)).max()
    if span<1e-3: return "fixed", sol
    v=tail[1]; pk=_peaks(v)
    if len(pk)<4: return "periodic", sol
    iv=np.diff(pk)*dt; ht=v[pk]
    cv_iv=np.std(iv)/(np.mean(iv)+1e-9); cv_ht=np.std(ht)/(abs(np.mean(ht))+1e-9)
    if cv_iv<0.06 and cv_ht<0.06: return "periodic", sol
    return "chaotic", sol

def lyap_max(rhs, params, x0=X0, t_end=250, dt=0.2, d0=1e-7):
    x=np.array(x0,float); xp=x+d0*np.ones(5)/np.sqrt(5); s=0.0; n=int(t_end/dt)
    for _ in range(n):
        for st in (x,xp):
            r=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,dt),st,args=params,method="RK45",rtol=1e-8,atol=1e-10)
            st[:]=r.y[:,-1]
        diff=xp-x; dist=np.linalg.norm(diff)
        if dist==0 or not np.isfinite(dist): break
        s+=np.log(dist/d0); xp=x+diff*(d0/dist)
    return s/(n*dt)
```

##### 4. Regime maps (the headline figure)

Sweep two parameters, colour each cell by regime. This turns Lin's "we sampled 300
random points" into an actual **map of where each behavior lives**.

- **Lin:** sweep `c` (isG) vs `h` (R). Others = 1.
- **Saturating:** sweep `c` (Sheng) vs `h` (Ke restraint). a=2,k=0.5,g=0.5.

`N=7` runs in ~1-2 min. Bump N to 11-15 for a finer map.

**Cell 9 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Colab.ipynb`):**

```python
REGIME_COLORS={"fixed":0,"periodic":1,"chaotic":2,"blowup":3,"fail":3}
CMAP=plt.matplotlib.colors.ListedColormap(["#4c72b0","#55a868","#c44e52","#cccccc"])
LABELS=["fixed","periodic","chaotic","blowup/fail"]

def sweep(rhs, base, sweep_specs):
    (pi,pvals),(qi,qvals)=sweep_specs
    grid=np.zeros((len(qvals),len(pvals)))
    for iy,qv in enumerate(qvals):
        for ix,pv in enumerate(pvals):
            params=list(base); params[pi]=pv; params[qi]=qv
            reg,_=classify(rhs,tuple(params))
            grid[iy,ix]=REGIME_COLORS.get(reg,3)
    return grid

N=7
c_vals=np.linspace(0.3,2.5,N); h_vals=np.linspace(0.0,1.6,N)

print("sweeping Lin model...")
grid_lin=sweep(rhs_lin,[1,1,1,1,1,1],((2,c_vals),(5,h_vals)))
print("sweeping saturating model...")
grid_sat=sweep(rhs_sat,[2,0.5,1,0.5,0.3],((2,c_vals),(4,h_vals)))

fig,axes=plt.subplots(1,2,figsize=(13,5))
for ax,grid,title in [(axes[0],grid_lin,"Lin (bilinear)"),
                      (axes[1],grid_sat,"Saturating (yours)")]:
    im=ax.imshow(grid,origin="lower",aspect="auto",cmap=CMAP,vmin=0,vmax=3,
                 extent=[c_vals[0],c_vals[-1],h_vals[0],h_vals[-1]])
    ax.set_xlabel("c  (generation / Sheng strength)")
    ax.set_ylabel("h  (Ke restraint strength)"); ax.set_title(title)
cbar=fig.colorbar(im,ax=axes,ticks=[0.4,1.1,1.9,2.6]); cbar.ax.set_yticklabels(LABELS)
plt.suptitle("Regime maps: where fixed / periodic / chaotic live"); plt.show()
```

For each cell it overwrites two entries of the parameter list, calls `classify`, and stores the class as an integer 0–3. `imshow` then draws the integer grid with a 4-colour list (blue = fixed, green = periodic, red = chaotic, grey = blow-up/fail); `extent` puts the true parameter values on the axes, and `origin='lower'` makes h increase upward. Two panels, one per model, share one colour bar whose tick labels are replaced by the class names.

grey = blow-up. The saturating model should show a large blue (stable) region the Lin
map lacks — the central comparison. Green bands are the periodic solutions.

##### 5. Auto-pick clean examples from each regime

**Cell 12 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Colab.ipynb`):**

```python
def find_example(rhs, base, sweep_specs, target):
    (pi,pvals),(qi,qvals)=sweep_specs
    for qv in qvals:
        for pv in pvals:
            params=list(base); params[pi]=pv; params[qi]=qv
            reg,sol=classify(rhs,tuple(params))
            if reg==target:
                sol=integrate(rhs,tuple(params),t_end=400,dt=0.05)
                return tuple(params),sol
    return None,None

def show_example(rhs,params,sol,title):
    fig,ax=plt.subplots(1,2,figsize=(12,4)); t=sol.t; y=sol.y
    for i in range(5): ax[0].plot(t,y[i],color=COLORS[i],label=NAMES[i],lw=0.9)
    ax[0].set_xlabel("time"); ax[0].set_ylabel("x_i"); ax[0].legend(ncol=5,fontsize=7)
    ax[0].set_title(title+" — time series")
    n0=int(len(t)*0.5)
    ax[1].plot(y[1,n0:],y[2,n0:],lw=0.5,color="purple")
    ax[1].set_xlabel("Fire"); ax[1].set_ylabel("Earth"); ax[1].set_title("phase (settled)")
    plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

for reg in ["periodic","chaotic"]:
    p,sol=find_example(rhs_lin,[1,1,1,1,1,1],((2,c_vals),(5,h_vals)),reg)
    if sol is not None: show_example(rhs_lin,p,sol,f"LIN {reg} (c,h)=({p[2]:.2f},{p[5]:.2f})")
    else: print(f"Lin: no {reg} in swept range")

for reg in ["fixed","periodic"]:
    p,sol=find_example(rhs_sat,[2,0.5,1,0.5,0.3],((2,c_vals),(4,h_vals)),reg)
    if sol is not None: show_example(rhs_sat,p,sol,f"SATURATING {reg} (c,h)=({p[2]:.2f},{p[4]:.2f})")
    else: print(f"Saturating: no {reg} in swept range")
```

##### What you can write about from this notebook

- **Regime-map comparison:** the saturating model has an extended region of stable
  equilibria (health) absent from the bilinear model — the core result, one figure.
- **Periodic solutions as physiological rhythms:** the green bands are sustained
  oscillations (cyclic waxing/waning of organ activity).
- **Boundedness:** where Lin turns grey (blow-up/extinction) the saturating model does
  not — its couplings keep the system physiological.
- **Disease onset:** a path from blue across a boundary into green/red is the model's
  picture of leaving health; that boundary is where you'd run AUTO for the exact
  bifurcation.
- **Correction to Lin:** their "periodic" case has largest Lyapunov ~0 (true limit
  cycle); their "chaos" case is conservative (no attractor).


# Part 5 — Colab notebook 2: stability comparison

**Your request:** "Shouldn't you use the code you made previously [the variational Lyapunov code]? … for now I want to write and show a **stability comparison of Lin and our model**; give me Colab code. It should be real … and show that our model is stable at the specific parameters while Lin's paper model doesn't really work. Also with what you said previously, implement and fix."

**What the notebook does.**

1. Defines both models, their balanced states (closed form) and analytic Jacobians.
2. **Eigenvalues** of the Jacobian at the balanced state: stability ⇔ every eigenvalue has negative real part.
3. **Perturbation recovery**: knock one organ (small = +5% of x\*; severe = scaled to 30% of x\*) and watch whether the five organs return to x\*.
4. **Stability maps** over (c, h): for each cell recompute x\* and the Jacobian and colour by max Re λ.

This is the notebook that gives the first headline: Lin's baseline is unstable (+0.206) and ours is stable (−1.154). The Lyapunov-exponent discussion was also corrected here to use the variational method of Part 3 rather than the crude two-trajectory estimator.

#### Stability of the Balanced State: Lin (bilinear) vs Saturating Model

**Claim to be demonstrated:** the saturating model has a *stable* balanced
(healthy) equilibrium; the bilinear Lotka--Volterra model of Lin & Han does not.

Four independent lines of evidence:
1. **Equilibria** — closed forms for both models.
2. **Eigenvalues** — plotted in the complex plane; stability = all in the left half.
3. **Perturbation recovery** — nudge one organ and watch what happens (the physical test).
4. **Stability maps** — where in parameter space each model is stable.

Run top to bottom (Runtime -> Run all). ~1-2 minutes, nothing to upload.

**Cell 1 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

NAMES=["Wood","Fire","Earth","Metal","Water"]
COLORS=["green","red","magenta","black","blue"]
```

##### 1. The two models

Same graph, different coupling forms. Index maps via `np.roll`:
mother `i-1`, controller `i-2`, child `i+1`, target `i+2`.

**Lin (bilinear):** $\dot x_i = ax_i-bx_i^2+cx_ix_{i-1}-dx_ix_{i-2}-gx_ix_{i+1}-hx_ix_{i+2}$

**Saturating (ours):** $\dot x_i = ax_i-kx_i^2+\frac{cx_{\sigma(i)}}{1+x_i}-\frac{gx_i}{1+x_{\kappa(i)}}-hx_ix_{\rho(i)}$

**Cell 3 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
# ---------- LIN bilinear ----------
def rhs_lin(t,x,a,b,c,d,g,h):
    x=np.asarray(x)
    return (a*x-b*x**2 + c*x*np.roll(x,1) - d*x*np.roll(x,2)
            - g*x*np.roll(x,-1) - h*x*np.roll(x,-2))

def eq_lin(a,b,c,d,g,h):
    """Lin's symmetric equilibrium a/(b+d+g+h-c) (their eq. 7)."""
    M=b+d+g+h-c
    return a/M if M>0 else np.nan

def jac_lin(xv,a,b,c,d,g,h):
    J=np.zeros((5,5))
    for i in range(5):
        im1,im2,ip1,ip2=(i-1)%5,(i-2)%5,(i+1)%5,(i+2)%5
        J[i,i]=a-2*b*xv[i]+c*xv[im1]-d*xv[im2]-g*xv[ip1]-h*xv[ip2]
        J[i,im1]+=c*xv[i]; J[i,im2]+=-d*xv[i]
        J[i,ip1]+=-g*xv[i]; J[i,ip2]+=-h*xv[i]
    return J

# ---------- SATURATING (ours) ----------
def rhs_sat(t,x,a,k,c,g,h):
    x=np.asarray(x)
    return (a*x-k*x**2 + c*np.roll(x,1)/(1+x)
            - g*x/(1+np.roll(x,-2)) - h*x*np.roll(x,2))

def eq_sat(a,k,c,g,h):
    """Positive root of (k+h)x^2 + [(k+h)-a]x - (a+c-g) = 0."""
    A=k+h; B=(k+h)-a; C=-(a+c-g)
    disc=B*B-4*A*C
    if disc<0: return np.nan
    r=(-B+np.sqrt(disc))/(2*A)
    return r if r>0 else np.nan

def jac_sat(xv,a,k,c,g,h):
    J=np.zeros((5,5))
    for i in range(5):
        im1,ip2,im2=(i-1)%5,(i+2)%5,(i-2)%5
        mo,ta,co=xv[im1],xv[ip2],xv[im2]
        J[i,i]=a-2*k*xv[i]-c*mo/(1+xv[i])**2-g/(1+ta)-h*co
        J[i,im1]+=c/(1+xv[i])
        J[i,ip2]+=g*xv[i]/(1+ta)**2
        J[i,im2]+=-h*xv[i]
    return J
```

##### 2. Equilibria and eigenvalues

Baseline parameters: Lin at $a=b=c=d=g=h=1$ (their Case 1);
ours at $a=2,k=0.5,c=1,g=0.5,h=0.3$ (the table in our paper).

**Cell 5 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
P_LIN=(1,1,1,1,1,1)
P_SAT=(2,0.5,1,0.5,0.3)

xl=eq_lin(*P_LIN); Jl=jac_lin(np.full(5,xl),*P_LIN); el=np.linalg.eigvals(Jl)
xs=eq_sat(*P_SAT); Js=jac_sat(np.full(5,xs),*P_SAT); es=np.linalg.eigvals(Js)

print(f"LIN  x* = {xl:.6f}   (their reported 1/3)")
for e in sorted(el,key=lambda z:-z.real): print(f"     {e.real:+.4f}{e.imag:+.4f}i")
print(f"     max Re = {max(el.real):+.4f}  ->  {'UNSTABLE' if max(el.real)>0 else 'stable'}")

print(f"\nSAT  x* = {xs:.6f}   (closed form 3/4+sqrt(59)/4 = {0.75+np.sqrt(59)/4:.6f})")
for e in sorted(es,key=lambda z:-z.real): print(f"     {e.real:+.4f}{e.imag:+.4f}i")
print(f"     max Re = {max(es.real):+.4f}  ->  {'unstable' if max(es.real)>0 else 'STABLE'}")

print("\nJacobian of our model is circulant:",
      all(np.allclose(Js[i],np.roll(Js[0],i)) for i in range(5)))
w=np.exp(2j*np.pi/5); r0=Js[0]
lam=np.array([sum(r0[m]*w**(j*m) for m in range(5)) for j in range(5)])
print("closed-form DFT eigenvalues agree with numpy:",
      np.allclose(np.sort_complex(lam),np.sort_complex(es)))
```

###### Eigenvalue plot — the stability figure

Stability means **every** eigenvalue lies strictly left of the imaginary axis.

**Cell 7 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
fig,ax=plt.subplots(figsize=(7,5))
ax.axvline(0,color='k',lw=1)
ax.axhline(0,color='gray',lw=0.5,ls=':')
ax.fill_betweenx([-1.5,1.5],0,3,color='red',alpha=0.06)
ax.text(0.35,1.25,'unstable half-plane',color='red',fontsize=9)

ax.scatter(el.real,el.imag,s=110,marker='o',facecolor='none',
           edgecolor='crimson',lw=2,label='Lin (bilinear)')
ax.scatter(es.real,es.imag,s=110,marker='x',color='navy',lw=2,
           label='Saturating (ours)')

ax.set_xlabel(r'Re $\lambda$'); ax.set_ylabel(r'Im $\lambda$')
ax.set_title('Eigenvalues of the Jacobian at the balanced state')
ax.legend(loc='lower left'); ax.set_xlim(-2.6,0.9); ax.set_ylim(-1.4,1.4)
ax.grid(alpha=0.25); plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

print("Lin has two eigenvalues in the red (unstable) region; ours has none.")
```

The vertical black line at Re λ = 0 is the stability boundary and the red-shaded half-plane is the unstable region. Stability ⇔ all markers left of the line. ##### 3. Perturbation recovery — the physical test

Eigenvalues are a local statement. Here we test it directly: start at the balanced
state, nudge **one** organ, and integrate.

- **Small perturbation:** +5% on Wood.
- **Severe perturbation:** Wood reduced to 30% of balance (a deficiency pattern).

A stable model returns to balance; an unstable one does not.

**Cell 9 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
def perturb(rhs,params,xstar,mode="small",t_end=120):
    x0=np.full(5,xstar)
    if mode=="small": x0[0]+=0.05*xstar
    else:             x0[0]*=0.30
    sol=solve_ivp(rhs,(0,t_end),x0,args=params,method="RK45",
                  t_eval=np.linspace(0,t_end,3000),rtol=1e-9,atol=1e-11)
    dev=np.linalg.norm(sol.y-xstar,axis=0)
    return sol,dev

fig,axes=plt.subplots(2,2,figsize=(13,7))
for col,(nm,rhs,params,xstar) in enumerate(
        [("LIN (bilinear)",rhs_lin,P_LIN,xl),
         ("SATURATING (ours)",rhs_sat,P_SAT,xs)]):
    for row,mode in enumerate(["small","severe"]):
        sol,dev=perturb(rhs,params,xstar,mode)
        ax=axes[row,col]
        for i in range(5):
            ax.plot(sol.t,sol.y[i],color=COLORS[i],label=NAMES[i],lw=1.1)
        ax.axhline(xstar,color='gray',ls='--',lw=1)
        ax.set_title(f"{nm} — {mode} perturbation")
        ax.set_xlabel("time"); ax.set_ylabel("$x_i$")
        if row==0 and col==0: ax.legend(ncol=5,fontsize=7)
plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()

# quantitative summary
print(f"{'':22s}{'start dev':>10s}{'final dev':>12s}{'verdict':>14s}")
for nm,rhs,params,xstar in [("LIN small",rhs_lin,P_LIN,xl),
                            ("LIN severe",rhs_lin,P_LIN,xl),
                            ("SAT small",rhs_sat,P_SAT,xs),
                            ("SAT severe",rhs_sat,P_SAT,xs)]:
    mode="small" if "small" in nm else "severe"
    sol,dev=perturb(rhs,params,xstar,mode)
    verdict="recovers" if dev[-1]<1e-3 else "does NOT recover"
    print(f"{nm:22s}{dev[0]:10.4f}{dev[-1]:12.4f}{verdict:>18s}")
```

##### 4. Stability maps over parameter space

For each $(c,h)$ we recompute the equilibrium, its Jacobian, and
$\max_j \operatorname{Re}\lambda_j$. Blue = stable, red = unstable, and the black
contour is the **stability boundary** $\max\operatorname{Re}\lambda = 0$ — the
model's picture of the transition out of health.

**Cell 12 — code (verbatim from `WuXing_Stability_Comparison.ipynb`):**

```python
N=90
c_vals=np.linspace(0.05,2.5,N)
h_vals=np.linspace(0.05,2.0,N)

Mlin=np.full((N,N),np.nan); Msat=np.full((N,N),np.nan)
for iy,hv in enumerate(h_vals):
    for ix,cv in enumerate(c_vals):
        # Lin: vary c and h, others = 1
        xe=eq_lin(1,1,cv,1,1,hv)
        if np.isfinite(xe) and xe>0:
            Mlin[iy,ix]=max(np.linalg.eigvals(jac_lin(np.full(5,xe),1,1,cv,1,1,hv)).real)
        # Ours: vary c and h, a=2,k=0.5,g=0.5
        xe2=eq_sat(2,0.5,cv,0.5,hv)
        if np.isfinite(xe2) and xe2>0:
            Msat[iy,ix]=max(np.linalg.eigvals(jac_sat(np.full(5,xe2),2,0.5,cv,0.5,hv)).real)

fig,axes=plt.subplots(1,2,figsize=(13,5))
vmax=np.nanmax(np.abs([np.nanmin([Mlin,Msat]),np.nanmax([Mlin,Msat])]))
for ax,M,title in [(axes[0],Mlin,"Lin (bilinear)"),(axes[1],Msat,"Saturating (ours)")]:
    im=ax.imshow(M,origin="lower",aspect="auto",cmap="RdBu_r",
                 vmin=-vmax,vmax=vmax,
                 extent=[c_vals[0],c_vals[-1],h_vals[0],h_vals[-1]])
    CS=ax.contour(c_vals,h_vals,M,levels=[0],colors='k',linewidths=2)
    ax.clabel(CS,fmt={0:'stability boundary'},fontsize=8)
    ax.set_xlabel("c  (Sheng / generation strength)")
    ax.set_ylabel("h  (Ke restraint strength)")
    frac=100*np.nanmean(M<0)
    ax.set_title(f"{title}\nstable over {frac:.0f}% of the region")
    fig.colorbar(im,ax=ax,label=r"max Re $\lambda$")
plt.suptitle("Stability of the balanced state (blue = stable)")
plt.tight_layout(); plt.show()
```

##### 5. Summary

| | Lin (bilinear) | Saturating (ours) |
|---|---|---|
| balanced equilibrium | $a/(b+d+g+h-c)$ | positive root of $(k+h)x^2+[(k+h)-a]x-(a+c-g)$ |
| at baseline | $x^*=1/3$ | $x^*=\tfrac34+\tfrac{\sqrt{59}}4\approx2.6703$ |
| max Re $\lambda$ | $+0.206$ | $-1.154$ |
| verdict | **unstable** | **stable** |
| small perturbation | grows | decays |
| severe perturbation | organs collapse toward 0 | full recovery |

**Interpretation for the paper.** Health is a state the body *returns to* after a
disturbance — an asymptotically stable equilibrium. The bilinear model cannot
represent this at its symmetric state, so it has no mathematical notion of a healthy
baseline; the saturating model does. The difference traces to a single modeling
choice: replacing unbounded mass-action couplings with saturating, demand-limited
ones.
