# Part 14 — Rewriting the paper to the SURE template (status)

**Your latest paper request:** "Use these resources, examples, guide, and template. For the template, ignore section 2 Related Work because that's like for CS, and method will just be an explanation of the model." The resources: Huguet–Rinzel–Hupé (2014, *J. Vision*) as an example of a dynamical-systems paper, your JCNS manuscript, a writing-guide text, and `Template_for_SURE_DS_2026.pdf`.

**What the template requires** (read from the PDF just now):

1. Title, names, *Texas Southern University*, *August 2026*.
2. **Abstract:** 1–2 sentences of background; 1 sentence stating the research question; 1–2 sentences on what was done; 1–2 sentences of conclusion/significance.
3. **1 Introduction** (a longer abstract; background first, then the research question, approach, results, conclusion; citations next to claims; new terminology in italics or bold; one message per paragraph; use numbered lists, not bullet points).
4. ~~2 Related Work~~ (**omitted**, as you said).
5. **3 Method** (for you: an explanation of the model, enough to reproduce the work).
6. **4 Results** (figures and tables with large legible labels; the main message should be readable from the figures alone).
7. **5 Conclusion.**
8. **6 Discussion** (connect to other work; future work).
9. **7 Acknowledgments**, which for SURE-DS **must contain** the sentence: *"This work was conducted in the program SURE-DS 2026 (Summer Undergraduate Research in Data Science 2026), organized by the Center of Scientific Machine Learning in Material Science and supported by AFOSR grant FA9550-23-1-0725 and the College of Science, Engineering, and Technology of Texas Southern University."*
10. **References.**

**What the writing guide adds:** write like a newspaper, not a mystery; declarative section headings; figures first, then text built around them; the bottom line is repeated only in the abstract, introduction and discussion; the Method describes ideas before equations; mathematical detail goes to appendices.

**Status — honestly:** the rewrite is **not finished and nothing from it has been delivered.** I had drafted only a preamble, abstract, Introduction and Method (`t1.tex`, with the proposed declarative title *"Saturating interactions allow the five-element model of Chinese medicine to hold a stable state of health"*), and that working file did not survive between sessions. Still to write: Results (declarative subsection headings, figures first), Conclusion, Discussion, Acknowledgments (with the mandatory sentence), appendices (proofs; corrections to Lin's Lyapunov spectra; numerical validation). The existing `paper_v3` is the content base. The template's order puts **Conclusion before Discussion**, which differs from the guide's order; I will follow the template unless you say otherwise.

# Appendix C — The modelling guide (full text of the earlier answer)

## Wu-Xing Modeling, Network Motifs, and Bifurcation Analysis
### A Complete Working Guide

---

## PART I — What the literature does (and why your approach is defensible)

The five-element (Wu-Xing) system has been given continuous dynamical-system
treatments before. Knowing what they did tells you where your contribution sits.

**The dominant template is Lotka–Volterra (LV).** The 2025 *Advances in Continuous
and Discrete Models* paper and the earlier Lin–Han "Analysis of Dynamic Five-Element
Model" both build the five elements as an LV-type system with mutual promotion
(Sheng) and mutual restraint (Ke) terms, then hunt for fixed points, limit cycles,
and chaos. Lin–Han explicitly enumerate the influence types they include: "gain",
"saturation", "generating", "is-generated", "restricting", "is-restricted" — which is
essentially the same list of relations you wrote in your LaTeX, just named
differently. They report that depending on parameters the system goes to a fixed
point, a periodic orbit, or chaos, and they analyze stability with Lyapunov
exponents.

**Two things the literature does badly, which is your opening:**

1. **Parameter nihilism.** The Nian et al. paper you already flagged sets all the
   linear coefficients to 1 "which makes no sense." Several LV-Wuxing papers do
   something similar — they pick coefficients to *manufacture* chaos, then claim the
   chaos is biologically meaningful ("when the body is in a chaotic state, health is
   maintained"). This is backwards. Chaos is easy to produce in 5D LV systems and by
   itself says nothing about TCM.

2. **No connection back to clinical principles.** Almost none of them check whether
   the model reproduces the *named pathological relations* from the classics — 相乘
   (over-acting), 相侮 (rebellion), 母病及子 (mother-illness reaches child), 子盗母气
   (child steals mother's qi). They produce dynamics, not TCM.

**Your contribution, stated cleanly:** build a model whose *terms are individually
justified by a named TCM relation*, then show the model reproduces the classical
pathological principles as emergent behavior, and use bifurcation analysis to locate
the boundary between "healthy homeostasis" and "disease" in parameter space. The
motif framing (below) is what makes this publishable as more than "another LV
variant."

---

## PART II — The relations, and the term each one justifies

Here is the master list, merged from both your LaTeX files, with the exact
mathematical term each relation licenses and the reasoning.

### A. Self-regulation (the 1-node motif)

**Relation:** each organ produces its own qi and cannot grow without bound.

**Term:** `a·x_i − k·x_i²` (logistic).

**Reasoning:** `a·x_i` is self-production proportional to current activity (an organ
that is more active regenerates faster). `−k·x_i²` is crowding/saturation — a
carrying capacity `a/k`. This is the standard logistic self-loop and it is the
correct 1-node motif. **Keep it.** The only reason to change it is if you want the
*set point itself* to move (see the adaptive variant), which is a genuinely different
biological claim, not a cosmetic change.

### B. Sheng (生) — the generating / mother-child relations

The Sheng cycle is `x1→x2→x3→x4→x5→x1`, mother σ(i) feeds child i.
There are FIVE sub-relations here and they do NOT all get the same term:

1. **Mother nourishes child; child draws back on the mother.**
   Term in child: `+c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)`.
   Reasoning: nourishment is proportional to the mother's level `x_{σ(i)}`, but the
   `1/(1+x_i)` factor makes it *demand-driven* — when the child is already full
   (x_i large) it draws little; when the child is depleted (x_i small) the term is
   large. This is the "back-draw" and it is what lets weakness propagate correctly.

2. **Deficiency in mother → deficiency in child (母病及子).**
   Already captured by term 1: if `x_{σ(i)}` drops, the nourishment `c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)`
   drops, so the child falls. No new term needed.

3. **Excess in mother → excess in child.**
   Same term, opposite direction. No new term.

4. **Excess in child → overdraw from mother (child uses qi fast, mother must
   replenish; equivalently 子盗母气 — child steals mother's qi).**
   Term in MOTHER: `−c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)` appears with a *matching consumption* on
   the mother's equation. If the child pulls `+c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)`, conservation
   suggests the mother loses a corresponding `−g·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)` (rate g need not
   equal c — nourishing is not perfectly efficient). THIS IS THE TERM MOST MODELS
   OMIT, and it is what makes the mother-child coupling bidirectional.

5. **Weakness in child eventually weakens mother.**
   This is the long-time consequence of relation 4 — no separate term, it is the
   integrated effect of the mother's consumption term.

**Key modeling decision.** In your current `chineseOrgans.tex` full model the Sheng
term is written `+c/(1+x_i)·x_{σ(i)}` in the child but there is no matching
consumption term in the mother — the mother nourishes "for free." That breaks
relations 4 and 5. If you want the full mother-child pathology, you must ALSO put
`−g_mother·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)` (or the `x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)` form) into the mother's
equation. Your "Exploring the modeling" section already writes this correct version
— it just never made it into the main model. **Recommendation: promote it.**

### C. Ke (克) — the controlling relations

The Ke cycle is `x1⊣x3⊣x5⊣x2⊣x4⊣x1`. For organ i:
- κ(i) = the organ i controls (its target)
- ρ(i) = the organ that controls i (its controller)

There are THREE distinct Ke sub-relations:

6. **Normal restraint.** Controller ρ(i) suppresses i.
   Term: `−h·x_i·x_{ρ(i)}` (mass-action competition, LV-style).
   Reasoning: suppression needs both parties present — no controller, no restraint;
   nothing to restrain, no effect. Product form is correct.

7. **Ke over-acting / 相乘 (贼邪).** If the controller is in excess OR the target is
   deficient, suppression becomes pathologically strong.
   Term: `−g·x_i/(1+x_{κ(i)})`.
   Reasoning: this is the drain on i associated with controlling a *weak* target.
   When the target `x_{κ(i)}` is low, `1/(1+x_{κ(i)})` → 1 and the drain is maximal;
   when the target is healthy the drain saturates away. This encodes "deficiency in
   the target amplifies the controlling cost." (An alternative, arguably cleaner,
   encoding of 相乘 puts the ratio `x_{ρ(i)}/x_i` directly into i's suppression — see
   Part V "cleaner alternatives.")

8. **Wu / rebellious counter-acting / 相侮 (微邪).** If the target is in excess OR the
   controller is deficient, the normally-controlled organ rebels back on its
   controller.
   Term (in the controller i): `−b·x_{κ(i)}/(1+ε·x_i)`.
   Reasoning: when i (the controller) is weak (x_i small), `1/(1+ε·x_i)` → 1 and the
   target `x_{κ(i)}` drains i in reverse. When i is strong the rebellion is
   suppressed. ε sets how deficient i must get before rebellion kicks in. This is the
   "microbe/微邪" mild reverse suppression.

### D. Adaptive set point (optional, a genuinely different model)

**Relation:** an organ's *healthy target level* is not constitutional-fixed but is
raised by a well-fed mother and lowered by a strong controller.

**Term:** replace `a·x_i − k·x_i²` with `k·x_i·(θ_i − x_i)`, where
`θ_i = θ0 + α·x_{σ(i)} − β·x_{ρ(i)}`.

**Reasoning:** this says homeostasis is *contextual* — the same organ "wants" to sit
higher when its mother is strong and lower when its controller is strong. It is a
real, testable alternative hypothesis to fixed logistic self-regulation, and it is
worth a separate model precisely because it changes the equilibrium structure (the
set point now depends on neighbors, so the symmetric equilibrium shifts and can
destabilize differently).

---

## PART III — The recommended model

Putting the justified terms together, the **full model** is:

    ẋ_i = a·x_i − k·x_i²                          (self, 1-node motif)
         + c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)                      (Sheng nourishment, child draws)
         − g_s·x_i/(1+x_{σ⁻¹(i)})                  (mother's consumption: child steals qi) [SEE NOTE]
         − g·x_i/(1+x_{κ(i)})                      (Ke over-acting 相乘)
         − h·x_i·x_{ρ(i)}                          (Ke normal restraint)
         − b·x_{κ(i)}/(1+ε·x_i)                    (Wu rebellion 相侮)

NOTE on the consumption term: σ⁻¹(i) is the CHILD of i (the organ i is mother to).
The consumption i suffers is proportional to how much its own child is drawing.
σ⁻¹: 1→2, 2→3, 3→4, 4→5, 5→1. So organ 1's child is 2, etc. This is the term that
makes Sheng bidirectional. If you leave it out you have the simpler model you're
running now, which is fine as a *baseline* but cannot show 子盗母气.

**This is why you have several model files** — each is the full model with one
mechanism switched off, so you can attribute any behavioral change to that one
mechanism:

| Model | Switch | Question it answers |
|-------|--------|--------------------|
| Full | — | reference behavior |
| No back-draw | `c·x_{σ(i)}/(1+x_i)` → `c_lin·x_{σ(i)}` | does demand-driven nourishment matter? |
| No Wu | drop `−b·…` | is rebellion needed for the interesting dynamics? |
| Adaptive | logistic → `k·x_i·(θ_i−x_i)` | does contextual homeostasis change stability? |
| 2-node | freeze 3 organs | what does the isolated motif do? |

They are NOT supposed to behave the same — the *differences* are your results.

---

## PART IV — Network motifs (the framing for main2.tex)

This is the part that turns "another LV model" into a paper with a thesis. Here is
what you need to know and what you can write about.

### What a network motif is

A network motif is a small subgraph pattern (2–4 nodes) that recurs in a larger
network far more often than in a randomized network with the same degree sequence.
The concept comes from Milo, Shen-Orr, Alon and collaborators (Science 2002; Nat.
Rev. Genet. 2007). Their central claims:

- Real biological networks (gene regulation in E. coli and yeast, neuronal wiring,
  metabolic networks) are built from a small vocabulary of recurring motifs.
- Each motif is associated with a characteristic **dynamical function** — it is a
  little circuit that does a job (filtering, delay, pulse generation, bistability).
- So you can often understand a big network by understanding its motifs plus how
  they are wired together.

The canonical motifs and their functions:

- **Autoregulation (self-loop).** Negative autoregulation speeds up response time and
  reduces noise; positive autoregulation can create bistability/memory.
- **Cascade** (A→B→C): signal propagation with delay.
- **Coherent feed-forward loop (FFL):** sign-consistent two paths from input to
  output; acts as a *sign-sensitive delay* / persistence detector (filters out brief
  input blips).
- **Incoherent FFL:** the two paths have opposite sign; acts as a *pulse generator*
  and a high-pass filter / accelerator; can give non-monotonic responses.
- **Feedback loops:** negative feedback → homeostasis and oscillation; positive
  feedback → switches and bistability.
- **Bi-fan:** two regulators jointly controlling two targets; combinatorial control.

### Why this framing fits Wu-Xing especially well

This is your actual argument, and it is genuinely novel-sounding:

**TCM diagnosis and treatment principles are stated as MOTIFS, not as whole-network
statements.** Every classical rule names 2 or 3 organs and a relation between them:

- 虚则补其母 ("if deficient, tonify the mother") is a statement about a **2-node
  mother→child edge**.
- 实则泻其子 ("if excess, drain the child") — same 2-node Sheng edge, other direction.
- 母能令子虚 / 子能令母实 — the 2-node Sheng edge again, pathological direction.
- The over-acting/rebellion rules 相乘/相侮 are statements about a **2-node Ke edge**.
- "Strengthen the healthy organ that the ill organ will attack next, before treating
  the sick one" is a statement about a **3-node motif**: ill → (Ke) → next-victim,
  with the mother edge feeding the victim (your Figure 3 in main2.tex is exactly
  this).

So TCM is *implicitly a motif theory of physiology*. The classics reason locally
(2–3 organs at a time) and assume those local circuits are approximately isolable on
the timescale of a single intervention. That is precisely the motif assumption —
and it is testable.

**The time-scale point (write this — it is the key justification).** A motif's
dynamics only "show through" in the full network if the motif is approximately
decoupled — either isolated, or receiving slowly-varying input from the rest. Your
main2.tex already says this: TCM implicitly assumes separation of time scales
(disease takes *time* to spread along an edge). So the local-motif reasoning of the
classics is mathematically justified *if and only if* the coupling to the rest of the
Wuxing network is weak or slow relative to the within-motif dynamics. This is a
concrete, checkable modeling claim and it is the backbone of the "local vs global
motifs" paper.

### Things you can write about (motif section checklist)

1. **Catalogue the Wuxing motifs.** 1-node (self-regulation), 2-node Sheng
   (generation), 2-node Ke (restraint), 3-node (mother + controller of a shared
   target — your Fig 3), and the two 5-cycles (Sheng pentagon, Ke pentagram) as the
   two global motifs.
2. **Map each classical principle to its motif.** The table above. This is the
   conceptual heart.
3. **Give each motif its own small ODE system and analyze it** (this is where the
   2-node XPP files come in). Show the 2-node Sheng motif reproduces 母病及子 and
   子盗母气; show the 2-node Ke motif reproduces 相乘/相侮.
4. **Local-to-global question.** When does the isolated-motif behavior survive
   embedding in the full 5-node network? Frame it as time-scale separation and test
   it: run the 2-node motif with frozen neighbors vs. the full model and compare.
5. **Motif combination.** How the Sheng pentagon and Ke pentagram overlap on the
   same 5 nodes (each node is in one of each) — this overlap is what makes the global
   dynamics richer than any single motif.
6. **Statistical-motif aside (optional, honest).** The classical Milo definition
   ("occurs more than in a randomized network") is about *large* networks. Wuxing is
   a single fixed 5-node graph, so you are using "motif" in the functional-building-
   block sense (Alon's design-principles sense), not the over-representation-
   statistics sense. Say so explicitly — it pre-empts a referee objection.

Key references to cite: Milo et al. 2002 (Science) for the definition; Alon 2007
(Nat. Rev. Genet.) and Alon's *Introduction to Systems Biology* for motif functions;
Mangan & Alon 2003 (PNAS) for the FFL-as-delay result; Shen-Orr et al. 2002 (Nat.
Genet.) for the E. coli motifs.

---

## PART V — Cleaner alternatives worth considering

You asked whether things should change. Two suggestions where the current terms are
defensible but a cleaner encoding exists:

**1. Encode 相乘 as a ratio.** The classics say over-acting scales with the *ratio*
of controller to target — "excess of controller OR deficiency of target." Your
`−g·x_i/(1+x_{κ(i)})` captures "deficiency of target" but not "excess of controller"
in the same term. A single term `−g·x_i·x_{ρ(i)}/(η + x_i)` (controller over target)
captures both at once. This is optional — the split `h` + `g` terms also cover it
between them.

**2. Make ε explicit as a threshold, not a slope.** In the Wu term
`−b·x_{κ(i)}/(1+ε·x_i)`, ε currently controls how fast rebellion shuts off as i
grows. If you want a genuine *threshold* θ_wu below which rebellion switches on, a
Hill form `−b·x_{κ(i)}·θ_wu^n/(θ_wu^n + x_i^n)` is sharper and gives you a clean
bifurcation parameter (n). Your AUTO `.f90` file with the Hill exponent `n_h` is
already set up for exactly this style — that is a good instinct.

---

## PART VI — Bifurcation analysis: what to actually do and look for

This is the part where you said "I didn't see anything interesting." That is usually
because (a) the system just sits at one stable symmetric equilibrium for all
parameter values you swept, or (b) you swept the wrong parameter, or (c) you never
broke the symmetry. Here is how to make it interesting.

### The core idea

A bifurcation is a *qualitative* change in the long-term behavior as a parameter
crosses a critical value: a stable equilibrium loses stability, two equilibria
collide and vanish, a limit cycle is born, etc. The bifurcation diagram plots the
location/stability of equilibria (and cycle amplitudes) against the parameter. The
"interesting" points are where stability changes.

### Why your symmetric system looks boring

Your model has **rotational symmetry**: all five organs share parameters and the
cyclic wiring is identical. That forces a **symmetric equilibrium**
x1=x2=x3=x4=x5=x* that exists for essentially all parameter values and is often
stable across a wide range. If you only track that branch, the diagram is a boring
smooth curve with no bifurcations. **The interesting physics is where the symmetric
state loses stability** — that is the mathematical image of "the body leaves balanced
health and a specific organ pattern takes over (disease)."

### What to look for (in order of what will actually show up)

1. **Symmetry-breaking (pitchfork / branch point, "BP" in AUTO).** As you push a
   coupling parameter (try `g`, `h`, or `b`), the equal-organ equilibrium can become
   unstable and new *non-uniform* equilibria branch off, where some organs are high
   and others low. THIS IS THE MODEL'S PICTURE OF A DISEASE PATTERN — a specific
   excess/deficiency configuration. This is your headline result.

2. **Hopf bifurcation ("HB").** A complex-conjugate eigenvalue pair crosses the
   imaginary axis and a limit cycle is born — the organs start *oscillating*.
   Biologically: cyclic waxing/waning of organ activity. Cyclic 5-node systems with
   restraint loops are prone to this. Push `b` (Wu strength) or `h` up; rebellion and
   restraint are the destabilizing loops.

3. **Saddle-node / fold ("LP", limit point).** Two equilibria collide and annihilate
   — a healthy state can *disappear* abruptly as a parameter drifts, forcing a jump to
   a distant (pathological) state. This is the model's version of a sudden clinical
   decompensation, and if you find two folds bracketing a region you have
   **bistability + hysteresis**: the system can be tipped into disease and not
   recover just by reversing the parameter (explains why prevention beats cure).

4. **Period-doubling → chaos** (only if you push hard). The LV-Wuxing literature
   reports this. It is real but be careful claiming clinical meaning — better to
   report it and stay agnostic than to over-interpret like the papers you criticized.

### Concrete recipe for getting a non-boring diagram

- **Pick a coupling parameter, not `a`.** Sweeping `a` (self-production) mostly just
  rescales the equilibrium — smooth and boring, which is what you saw. Sweep the
  *interaction* strengths: `g` (over-acting), `h` (restraint), `b` (rebellion), or the
  Hill exponent `n_h`. These are what destabilize balance.
- **Widen the range.** Go from 0 to several times the default. Bifurcations often sit
  outside the timid default window.
- **Two-parameter continuation.** Once you find a bifurcation point in 1 parameter,
  continue IT in a second parameter (AUTO does this: grab the HB/BP/LP point, then run
  with two free parameters). This traces a *curve* in the (g,h) or (b,ε) plane
  separating "healthy/stable" from "diseased/unstable." **That boundary curve is a far
  more interesting figure than any single 1-parameter diagram** — it is literally a
  map of where health lives in parameter space.
- **Break the symmetry deliberately.** Add a small perturbation to ONE organ's
  parameter (e.g. `a_3 = a + δ`, simulating a constitutionally weak spleen) and watch
  how the bifurcation structure changes. This connects directly to TCM
  constitution/pre-disease.

### What each variant model should reveal under bifurcation

- **No Wu vs Full:** does removing rebellion delete the Hopf? If the oscillations/
  chaos vanish without Wu, you've shown 相侮 is the destabilizing mechanism — a clean,
  quotable result.
- **No back-draw vs Full:** does demand-driven nourishment shift where symmetry
  breaks? Tests whether the mother-child feedback protects or endangers balance.
- **Adaptive vs Full:** does contextual homeostasis widen or narrow the stable
  (healthy) region? A widened region = adaptation is stabilizing.
- **2-node:** you can do this one *by hand* (nullclines, 2×2 Jacobian trace/
  determinant) and get analytic bifurcation conditions — the cleanest results in the
  paper, and they anchor the motif story.

### Practical XPPAUT/AUTO checklist

1. Integrate to settle, `Sing pts → Go` to land the equilibrium.
2. Open AUTO (`File → Auto`).
3. Parameter menu: set the bifurcation parameter (choose `g`, `h`, or `b`).
4. Axes → hI-Lo: y = an organ (or the L2 norm), x = the parameter.
5. Numerics: Par Min/Max = your wide range; Ds = 0.02, Dsmax = 0.5, Nmax = 500+.
6. Run → Steady state. Watch for BP/HB/LP labels.
7. **Grab** a special point, then run again to follow the branch that emerges from it
   (for BP: the symmetry-broken branch; for HB: the periodic branch — set
   Ntst≈50 first).
8. For a boundary map: grab the point, add a second free parameter (in the AUTO
   parameter window set two entries), Run → Two-param.

If Run → Steady state gives you a single point and stops, the starting equilibrium
did not satisfy F=0 at the AUTO parameters — re-settle in XPP, `Sing pts` again, then
retry. (That is exactly the situation you hit earlier.)

1. The two global motifs (Sheng pentagon, Ke pentagram) and the motif catalogue.
2. Table mapping each classical principle to its motif + model term.
3. Time series showing the model reproducing 母病及子, 子盗母气, 相乘, 相侮 (perturb one

4. 1-parameter bifurcation in `g` (or `h`): symmetric branch losing stability at a BP,
   symmetry-broken (disease-pattern) branch emerging.
5. 1-parameter bifurcation in `b` showing a Hopf (oscillatory disease).
6. Two-parameter boundary curve in (g, h) or (b, ε): the "health region."
7. No-Wu vs Full comparison isolating what rebellion contributes.
8. 2-node analytic nullcline/phase-plane figures anchoring the motif results.


# Appendix D — Understanding the Lin paper (full text of the earlier answer)

## Understanding Lin & Han (2020) + Your Research Plan

---

## PART 1 — What the Lin paper actually says (in plain terms)

### The model

Lin & Han take the five elements and write one ODE per element. Every element
feels **six influences**, and their whole paper is about which combinations of these
six produce which long-term behavior. The six influences (their names) are:

| Symbol | Name | Sign | Meaning |
|--------|------|------|---------|
| a | gain | + | self-production, proportional to own level |
| b | saturation | − | self-limitation (the −b·x² term) |
| c | isG (is-generated) | + | the element is nourished by its **mother** |
| d | isR (is-restricted) | − | the element is suppressed by its **controller** |
| g | G (generating) | − | the **cost** to the element of nourishing its child |
| h | R (restricting) | − | the **cost** to the element of restraining its target |

Written out (symmetric case, all elements share a,b,c,d,g,h), 0-indexed x[0..4]:

    dx_i/dt = a·x_i − b·x_i²          (self: gain − saturation)
            + c·x_i·x_{i−1}          (isG: mother nourishes me)
            − d·x_i·x_{i−2}          (isR: controller suppresses me)
            − g·x_i·x_{i+1}          (G : I pay to nourish my child)
            − h·x_i·x_{i+2}          (R : I pay to restrain my target)

**The single most important structural fact:** every coupling is a **bilinear
(mass-action) cross term** `x_i · x_j`. There is no saturation on the interactions —
only on the self term. This is a classic **Lotka–Volterra (LV)** form. Keep this in
mind; it is the crack in the paper that your modified model exploits (Part 4).

### Index maps (matching your LaTeX exactly)

- mother     = i−1   (Sheng: mother → me)
- child      = i+1   (Sheng: me → child)
- controller = i−2   (Ke: controller → me)   [= ρ(i)]
- target     = i+2   (Ke: me → target)       [= κ(i)]

This is identical to your `chineseOrgans.tex` σ, κ, ρ maps. So Lin's model and yours
are the *same graph* — they differ only in the **functional form** of the couplings
(bilinear vs saturating). That is exactly what makes them comparable motif-by-motif.

### The results they report (and what they mean)

They tested 300 random parameter/initial-condition combinations and found the system
always ends in one of three behaviors: **fixed point, periodic orbit, or chaos.**
Their headline sub-results:

**Case 1 (a=b=c=d=g=h=1).** The symmetric equilibrium is x* = 1/3 for all elements.
Linearizing gives eigenvalues **−1, 0.206±0.634i, −0.539±0.392i**. Two have positive
real part → **the balanced state is unstable.** (We reproduced these to the decimal.)
Interpretation: with all influences equal and strong, "perfect balance" is a
tightrope — the system will not stay there.

**Case R&isR only (d=h=1, rest 0).** Pure restriction. Every element only loses,
so the field is `−x_i·(d·controller + h·target)` — everything decays, and at least
three elements go to zero. Dissipative (phase-space volume shrinks). Interpretation:
restriction alone is purely destructive; you need generation to sustain activity.

**Case G&isG only (c,g>0, rest 0).** Pure generation. If c=g the system is
**conservative** (phase volume constant); if c<g dissipative; if c>g volume expands.

**Periodic (all params=1, start off-equilibrium at (1,2,3,4,5)).** Converges to a
**limit cycle** with period **T ≈ 17.626** (we reproduced T = 17.626 exactly). The
five elements pulse in a staggered wave around the cycle.

**Chaos (c=g=1, rest 0, start (1,2,3,4,5)).** Because c=g, this is the *conservative*
generation case, and it produces **bounded irregular (chaotic) motion** — the phase
portraits are filled rounded triangles (their Fig 5, which we reproduced). Their
reported Lyapunov exponents 0.67, 0.66, −0.076, −0.079, −1.18 **sum to zero**,
consistent with conservativeness.

**Their stability conclusion:** every case they examined is unstable — no stable
attracting balanced state exists in this model. They flag this as unfinished and say
the model needs to be tied back to clinical TCM.

### The tools they use (learn these — you will reuse all of them)

1. **Fixed points + linearization.** Solve dx/dt = 0, form the Jacobian, read
   eigenvalues. Positive real part anywhere ⇒ unstable.
2. **Lyapunov exponents.** Measure exponential divergence of nearby trajectories.
   >0 ⇒ chaos; =0 ⇒ periodic/quasiperiodic/conservative; <0 ⇒ stable. They used an
   SVD-on-time-series method; the cleaner way is the **variational/QR method** (in
   `lyapunov_spectrum.py`).
3. **Phase-space volume (dissipativity).** Compute the divergence of the vector field
   `div F = Σ ∂F_i/∂x_i = trace(Jacobian)`. <0 shrinking (dissipative, has an
   attractor); =0 conservative; >0 expanding. For this model
   `div F = Σ(a − 2b·x_i) + (c−d−g−h)Σx_i`, so the sign is controlled by the
   parameter combination — that is how they get their c=g conservative result.

---

## PART 2 — Reproducing it (the code, and how to run)

Two scripts are included, both verified against the paper:

### `lin_reproduce.py`
Reproduces every numerical claim:
- Case 1 equilibrium and Jacobian eigenvalues (matches −1, 0.206±0.634i, −0.539±0.392i)
- Fig 2 (R&isR decay)
- Fig 3 (periodic, prints estimated period → 17.626)
- Fig 4/5 (chaotic time series + phase portraits)
- symbolic divergence check for conservativeness

Run:
    pip install numpy scipy matplotlib
    python lin_reproduce.py

It saves `fig2_RisR.png`, `fig3_periodic.png`, `fig4_chaos_timeseries.png`,
`fig5_chaos_phase.png`.

### `lyapunov_spectrum.py`
Computes the **full Lyapunov spectrum** by integrating the state plus 5 tangent
vectors and doing periodic QR reorthonormalization (the rigorous method).

Run:
    python lyapunov_spectrum.py

#### What we found (and a genuine research observation)
- **Periodic case:** largest exponent ≈ 0 (we get −0.001) → confirms a true limit
  cycle. NOTE: the paper reported a *positive* largest exponent (0.3885) for this
  same "periodic" case, which is inconsistent with a limit cycle. The variational
  method is more trustworthy here; **their SVD-time-series estimate is likely
  contaminated by the transient.** This is a small but legitimate correction you can
  make.
- **Chaos case:** the spectrum **sums to exactly 0** (conservative — robust and
  matches the paper), but the exponent *magnitudes* are highly method- and
  length-dependent. The conservative "chaos" here is delicate. **This is worth
  saying explicitly:** in a conservative (volume-preserving) 5D system, "chaos" is
  not the same as a chaotic attractor — there is no attractor at all, trajectories
  fill a constant-volume region. Over-claiming clinical meaning for this (as the
  literature does) is unjustified.

## PART 3 — Your five-step plan, fleshed out

You listed the right steps. Here is what each one concretely involves.

### Step 1 — Reproduce & understand Lin (DONE)
You now have working, verified code and the pure-math understanding above. Make sure
you can, by hand: write the Jacobian, explain each eigenvalue's meaning, state what
divergence tells you. These are the exact skills Steps 3–4 reuse.

### Step 2 — Look back at the articles
Re-read with these questions in mind (this becomes your Related Work section):
- Which of the six terms does each paper include? (Lin's Table-of-approaches
  paragraph is a ready-made map — [7,8,11] use all six; [16,17] use linear terms;
  [9–15] use cross terms.)
- Does the paper use **bilinear** couplings (LV) or **saturating** ones? Almost all
  use bilinear. Your saturating model is the point of difference.
- Does the paper ever get a **stable** balanced state? Mostly no. "Health = a stable
  equilibrium" is the gap.
- Does the paper connect to named clinical principles (相乘/相侮/母子)? Almost never.

### Step 3 — Apply the same analysis to your modified model
Take your saturating model and run the **identical pipeline**:
1. Find the symmetric equilibrium (numerically; your `find_eq` script).
2. Jacobian + eigenvalues at it → is it stable? (Prediction: for a range of
   parameters it IS stable, unlike Lin — that's your headline.)
3. Sweep a coupling parameter → bifurcation diagram (XPP/AUTO or Python).
4. Lyapunov spectrum where it looks non-stationary.
5. Divergence/dissipativity as a function of parameters.
Present it side-by-side with Lin's so the comparison is explicit.

### Step 4 — Network motifs of both models, compared
This is the intellectual core. Because both models live on the **same graph**, you
can compare them motif by motif:
- **1-node (self):** Lin `a·x − b·x²` vs yours (same, or adaptive set point).
- **2-node Sheng:** Lin `+c·x_i·x_{i−1}` (bilinear) vs yours
  `+c·x_{i−1}/(1+x_i)` (saturating, demand-driven). Show what each does to the
  mother-child pathologies. Lin's bilinear term makes nourishment unbounded in the
  mother's size; yours saturates → bounded, and encodes "weak child draws harder."
- **2-node Ke:** Lin `−d·x_i·x_{i−2}` and `−h·x_i·x_{i+2}` (both bilinear) vs yours
  `−g·x_i/(1+x_κ)` (over-acting) + `−h·x_i·x_ρ` + Wu term. Show that Lin cannot
  represent 相侮 (rebellion) at all — it has no term that reverses sign under
  deficiency — whereas yours does.
- **Comparison claim:** the terms are *analogous* (same edges, same signs at
  equilibrium) but the *functional responses* differ (LV type-I vs saturating
  type-II, in ecology language). Justify each substitution by the TCM relation it
  must capture (your guide Part II) and by boundedness/positivity (Part 4 below).

### Step 5 — Ties to other fields / applications
Real hooks, not hand-waving:
- **Ergodicity / conservative chaos.** Lin's c=g chaos is volume-preserving →
  connects to Hamiltonian/measure-preserving dynamics and ergodic theory. You can ask
  whether the filled phase region is ergodic (time average = space average). This is a
  clean, honest math aside and directly uses the divergence result.
- **Ecology (functional responses).** LV type-I vs Holling type-II responses is a
  60-year-old ecological distinction; framing Sheng/Ke as predator-prey-like
  interactions with saturating responses ties TCM modeling to mathematical ecology.
- **Systems biology / motifs.** Alon's design-principles program (Part 4 of the other
  guide) — TCM principles as functional motifs.
- **Control / treatment.** Once you have a stable healthy equilibrium and its basin,
  "treatment" = a control input (the e_i intervention terms Lin drops) that steers a
  diseased trajectory back into the healthy basin. This is a genuine application
  section.

---

## PART 4 — Why your modification is justified (write this carefully)

The reviewer will ask "why change Lin's model?" Here are the defensible reasons, in
order of strength:

1. **Boundedness & positivity (strongest).** Lin's bilinear model lets organs hit
   exactly zero (his R&isR case sends ≥3 elements to 0) or grow without saturation.
   An organ's qi going to zero = death; for a *living-body* model that is
   non-physical. Saturating couplings keep every trajectory positive and bounded,
   which is the correct state space for a living organism.

2. **Health as a STABLE equilibrium.** Lin proves his balanced state is always
   unstable — so his model literally cannot represent "sustained health." A model of
   medicine should have health as an attractor. Saturating self-limitation on the
   couplings restores a stable symmetric equilibrium over a physiological parameter
   range. **This is the single best argument.**

3. **Faithful encoding of named relations.** Bilinear terms cannot represent 相侮
   (rebellion switches on only under deficiency) or the demand-driven "weak child
   draws harder" (needs `1/(1+x_i)`). Your saturating/Hill terms can. So the modified
   model is *more faithful to the classics*, not just mathematically nicer.

4. **Interpretable bifurcations = disease onset.** With a stable healthy equilibrium,
   the bifurcation where it loses stability is a clean mathematical image of
   disease onset — something Lin's always-unstable model cannot provide.

### Where to KEEP Lin's choices
- Keep the **six-influence bookkeeping** and the **same graph** — it makes the
  comparison rigorous and shows you're building on, not ignoring, prior work.
- Keep **gain − saturation** self-regulation as the baseline (both models share it).
- Keep his **analysis pipeline** (eigenvalues, Lyapunov, divergence) — reusing it is
  what makes the comparison apples-to-apples.

---

## PART 5 — More things you could write about

Beyond your five steps, publishable additions:

1. **Symmetry & equivariant bifurcation theory.** The system has cyclic Z_5 symmetry.
   Z_5-equivariant bifurcation theory *predicts* the pattern of symmetry-broken
   branches (which organs go high/low together) and whether you get standing vs
   rotating-wave solutions. This is a rigorous, citable framework (Golubitsky–Stewart)
   and would set your paper apart mathematically. The staggered pulses in Fig 3 are
   a rotating wave — a Z_5 phenomenon.

2. **Two-parameter phase diagram of behavior.** Extend Lin's "300 random samples"
   into an actual map: for each (parameter, parameter) pair, classify the attractor
   (fixed/periodic/chaotic/blow-up) and color the plane. Do it for both models and
   compare how much of parameter space is "healthy" (stable fixed point).

3. **The intervention terms e_i as treatment.** Lin sets e_i=0 and forgets them.
   Reinstating them as controls and asking "what minimal e steers disease→health"
   turns the model into a treatment-design tool — directly matching the TCM principle
   of tonify-mother / drain-child as targeted inputs.

4. **Disease spreading along the Ke cycle.** Your main2.tex already wants this.
   Model an acute insult to one organ and track propagation; test the classical rule
   "strengthen the next victim before treating the sick organ" as a control strategy
   and show it reduces spread. This is a concrete, simulable clinical principle.

5. **Time-scale separation / motif isolability (rigorous).** Prove (or show
   numerically) that when inter-motif coupling is weak, the isolated 2-node motif
   dynamics approximate the embedded behavior — justifying why the classics can reason
   locally. Singular-perturbation / fast-slow analysis is the tool.

6. **Comparison table: functional responses.** A clean table contrasting bilinear
   (LV type I) vs saturating (type II) couplings for each TCM relation, with the
   qualitative consequence of each (boundedness, existence of stable equilibrium,
   ability to encode rebellion). Reviewers love a crisp comparison table.

7. **Robustness / constitution.** Break the symmetry by making one organ
   constitutionally weak (lower its a, or raise the h it receives) and show how the
   healthy region shrinks — a model of predisposition and "treating before illness."
